## 1. Follow the sky

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/track_sky_flow.dart; mobile/assets/ma_at_flows/*.md
- Overview: A Ma'at flow for skywatching in season. Choose your U.S. timezone and the remaining equinoxes, lunar events, meteor peaks, and planetary highlights through March 20, 2027 will be placed on your calendar with clear guidance on when to step outside, where to look, and what change to keep.

### Track Sky Narrative Guidance

#### penumbral lunar eclipse

- Tracking guidance: This is a quiet event. Look near the listed maximum and do not expect the drama of a red Moon. The change may be no more than a soft dimming, a faint unevenness across the lunar face. Let your eyes settle. The careful observer may see what the hurried eye misses.
- Ma'at reflection: Some signs are subtle by nature. Ma'at is not only in spectacle, but in attention.

#### partial lunar eclipse

- Tracking guidance: Watch the Moon before maximum, when the shadow is still gathering. One side of the disk will remain bright while the other takes on a muted gray, bronze, or red-brown tone. The sign is the curved edge of Earth's shadow. Stay with it long enough to see the balance shift across the face of the Moon.
- Ma'at reflection: Partial shadow still reveals order. Not every lesson arrives in total darkness.

#### total lunar eclipse

- Tracking guidance: Begin before the deepest hour of the eclipse, while the Moon is still bright enough to compare against its own shadow. Keep your attention on the edge of the disk. The change will come slowly: first a dimming, then a darkening, then the copper-red color of Earth's shadow crossing the Moon. Stay through the change rather than treating it as a glance-and-go event.
- Ma'at reflection: The Moon does not lose itself in shadow. It passes through, keeps its form, and returns with its measure intact. Its meaning is in the transformation.

#### equinox

- Tracking guidance: Observe the Sun at sunrise or sunset rather than chasing the exact clock-time of the equinox. Stand where you can see the horizon clearly. The Sun will rise close to due east and set close to due west, returning the year to a point of balance. Mark the place against a fixed landmark so the year can be measured by return, not memory alone.
- Ma'at reflection: The equinox is not a spectacle. It is a correction: light and dark brought back into relation.

#### solstice

- Tracking guidance: Watch the Sun through its path, especially at noon and near sunset. In summer, the day stretches long and the noon shadow shortens beneath the high Sun. In winter, the Sun travels low, the shadow lengthens, and darkness arrives early. The solstice is a limit: the far reach of light before the year begins to turn again.
- Ma'at reflection: Every force has a boundary. The wisdom is knowing when the turn has begun.

#### Meteor Showers

- Tracking guidance: Find a dark, open place after midnight and let the sky widen above you. Do not search with force. Let your eyes adjust, keep your phone away, and watch the whole sky rather than one fixed point. The meteors may appear as quick cuts of light, faint streaks, or sudden bright fire. Count them in quiet intervals and notice which direction they seem to come from.
- Ma'at reflection: The sky rewards patience. What appears sudden is still part of a larger order.

#### 6-planet parade

- Tracking guidance: Step out in evening twilight while the western horizon is still carrying the last light. Begin low in the west and let your gaze travel along the line of the ecliptic from one world to the next. Some planets will announce themselves at once; use binoculars and patience for the faintest.
- Ma'at reflection: When many wanderers briefly share one visible path, the sky shows agreement without stillness.

#### venus-jupiter conjunction

- Tracking guidance: Return to the western sky after sunset and give the low bright edge of dusk a little time. Venus and Jupiter will appear there close together, two steady lights holding one patch of sky. Watch their spacing against the fading light and the first stars. The event belongs to a few evenings, not a single instant, so let closeness reveal itself gradually.
- Ma'at reflection: Alignment can look like nearness. What matters is the order that lets two paths meet.

#### venus at greatest eastern elongation

- Tracking guidance: Look low toward the bright edge of dusk after sunset. Venus will stand there as a steady, brilliant light, brighter than the surrounding stars and carried well away from the Sun. Watch it across several evenings, noting how confidently it holds the western sky before drawing back in the weeks ahead.
- Ma'at reflection: Venus marks the threshold: the flame that remains after day has passed. Its lesson is position as well as brightness.

#### venus at greatest western elongation

- Tracking guidance: Look low toward the bright edge of dawn before sunrise. Venus will appear there as a steady, brilliant light, stronger than the nearby stars and clear of the Sun's glare. Watch it across several mornings, noting how it leads the day for a time before closing the distance again.
- Ma'at reflection: Venus marks the threshold: the light before light. Its lesson is movement as well as brightness.

#### jupiter at opposition

- Tracking guidance: Begin after sunset by looking east for the bright, steady planet rising into the night. Near midnight, it will stand higher toward the south; before dawn, it will move westward. Watch the whole arc rather than one moment, and notice how it holds steady against the turning sky.
- Ma'at reflection: A planet at opposition teaches presence. It stands fully revealed because it has come into right alignment.

#### saturn at opposition

- Tracking guidance: Begin after sunset by looking east for Saturn's steady golden light. Near midnight it will stand highest toward the south; before dawn it will move westward with the turning sky. Watch the whole arc rather than one moment. If you have a telescope, give the rings your attention in this season.
- Ma'at reflection: Saturn teaches structure through duration. What endures does not hurry to be seen, and Saturn rewards the longer watch.

#### mars at opposition

- Tracking guidance: Begin after sunset and look east for the reddish planet rising into the night. As the hours deepen it will stand stronger and clearer, and by dawn it will have begun its westward lean. Watch how its color separates it from the surrounding stars and how much more insistent it feels when the night is fully open.
- Ma'at reflection: Mars teaches force under measure. Brightness without discipline is only noise.

#### mercury at greatest western elongation

- Tracking guidance: Go out before sunrise while the eastern horizon is still narrow and dark. Mercury will remain low, brief, and close to the place where the Sun is coming. Find it early, keep your attention just above the horizon, and notice how quickly dawn begins to take it back. This is an event for the careful eye, not the hurried glance.
- Ma'at reflection: Some presences are measured by brevity. What is hardest to keep in view often sharpens attention.

#### micromoon

- Tracking guidance: Step out near moonrise and let the eastern horizon do the work for you. The Moon will rise full there, but the micromoon quality is a matter of measure, not spectacle. Keep an earlier full Moon in mind, or compare it against the same landmark you use each month, and notice how quietly size can change while brightness remains.
- Ma'at reflection: Measure is not diminished by subtlety. Small differences still belong to the order of return.

#### supermoon

- Tracking guidance: Step out near moonrise, while the eastern horizon is still holding the last color of the day. The Moon will rise there, low and large, carrying its first color before it climbs into white. Give the first hour your attention: notice where it clears the horizon, what it rises behind, and how its brightness gathers as it climbs.
- Ma'at reflection: The full Moon teaches fullness without haste. What has been growing in silence now becomes visible.

#### full moon

- Tracking guidance: Step out near moonrise while the eastern horizon is still holding the last color of day. The Moon will rise full there, warm at the horizon before it settles into white higher up. Stay with the first hour: notice where it appears, what landmark receives it, and how the light changes as it climbs.
- Ma'at reflection: The full Moon teaches fullness without haste. What has been growing in silence now becomes visible.

# 2026–March 20, 2027 Sky Events Grid for U.S. Central Time (CT)

Ultra-Detailed User Guide – Expanded for maximum depth. All times are converted for the U.S. Central Time Zone unless marked as global/non-visible. Use CDT/CST automatically: U.S. DST started March 8, 2026, ended November 1, 2026, and starts again March 14, 2027. For rise/set, horizon altitude, and eclipse visibility, confirm your exact city in Stellarium/SkySafari/timeanddate because a timezone is not a single observing location. Data cross-checked against NASA, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels/Fred Espenak, American Meteor Society, EarthSky, In-The-Sky, and major astronomy references.

Universal Tips (apply to every event):
* Download Stellarium or SkySafari app → set your exact city/location for real-time rise/set + horizon view.
* Dark-sky spots: Use rural horizons far from city light domes — examples include Big Bend/West Texas, Ozarks dark-sky areas, Great Plains sites, lake-country rural parks, or any open dark site away from major metros.
* Naked eye for 90% of these; binoculars add magic for planets/moon details.
* Safety: Eclipse glasses ONLY for solar events.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked these for the 365-day calendar, Nile floods, and festivals tied to Sirius, equinox balance, and lunar cycles.

**Timezone accuracy note:** Exact astronomical instants are converted to CT. Visibility, rise/set, altitude, and horizon direction still depend on your exact city, especially for eclipses and low-horizon planets.

## Lunar Events (Full Moons, Supermoons, Eclipses) – Kemetic Festival Timers

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Central Time (CT)

All times are converted for U.S. Central Time. Data cross-checked from NASA, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels/Fred Espenak, Griffith Observatory-style local visibility tools, and USNO-style astronomical timing references.

Universal Lunar Tips (apply to every event):
* Full moons rise near sunset and stay visible all night — easy naked-eye tracking.
* Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to CT) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Dark skies best (Joshua Tree or Anza-Borrego for max brightness/color). Binoculars reveal subtle red tones in eclipses or surface details.
* No equipment needed for any of these — just clear eastern/western horizon.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians used full moons and eclipses for festival timing, ritual offerings, and tracking seasonal Nile cycles (lunar calendar synced with solar/Sirius events).

The 2026–early 2027 span gives the Central Time Zone a strong pre-dawn lunar eclipse in March 2026, a strong overnight partial lunar eclipse in August 2026, and a subtle moonrise penumbral event in February 2027.

## Meteor Showers – Naked-Eye Fireworks from Comet Dust

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Central Time (CT)

Here’s the exact same depth and style as the Solar and Lunar grids — every entry packed with precise CT viewing windows, scientific breakdowns, what you’ll actually see in the sky, practical U.S.-timezone tips, rarity/significance, Kemetic ties, and notes. Meteor timing is inherently less “to-the-minute” than eclipses or phases; peaks are forecasts and the best actual viewing window is usually the darkest pre-dawn period nearest the peak.

Universal Meteor Tips (apply to every event):
* Best after midnight when the radiant (origin point) is highest — rates double or triple by pre-dawn.
* Lie flat on a reclining chair or blanket, face the radiant direction, and let your eyes dark-adapt for 20–30 minutes (no phone screens or white flashlights — use red light only).
* Dark skies are everything in CT: light pollution cuts rates dramatically. Drive 45–90 minutes to Joshua Tree, Anza-Borrego, or high desert spots for 5–10× more meteors. Binoculars optional for faint trains.
* Track your personal “hourly rate” by counting every 15 minutes — fun way to compare to the predicted zenithal hourly rate (ZHR).
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians were master sky observers but focused more on fixed stars/planets than fleeting meteors. Falling stars were sometimes interpreted as divine messages or “tears of the gods,” adding to their ritual sky-watching tradition alongside Sirius and lunar cycles.

| Event | Exact CT Date & Time | Scientific Breakdown | What You'll See & How to Track | Best Viewing Practices for CT | Rarity / Significance / Kemetic Tie | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lyrids | Peak best: after midnight April 21 into pre-dawn April 22, 2026; forecast maximum around daytime Apr 22 CT, so night viewing is key | Dust left by Comet Thatcher meets Earth’s path, producing swift meteors with occasional bright fireballs. | Short, fast streaks may appear from the northeast near Lyra, but meteors can cross any part of the sky. | Go out after midnight and stay until pre-dawn if possible. Lie back with your feet roughly northeast. Use naked eye only; let your eyes dark-adapt. | An annual shower of moderate strength; notable for occasional fireballs and clean spring observing. | Future. |
| Eta Aquariids | Peak best: pre-dawn May 5, 2026, with May 4 and May 6 also useful; forecast maximum occurs before the radiant is well placed for most U.S. observers | Dust from Halley’s Comet enters Earth’s atmosphere as very fast meteors, often with persistent trains. | Swift meteors rise from the southeast near Eta Aquarii, low in the sky for U.S. observers. They may leave brief glowing trains. | Go out in the final pre-dawn hours, ideally after 3 AM. Face southeast but keep your gaze wide. Avoid phones and city light; the Moon will reduce counts. | A Halley-linked shower always carries weight. In a Kemetic frame it suits broader comet and omen tracking. | Future. Corrected: the Moon is waning gibbous, not waxing gibbous, so reduced counts are expected. |
| Perseids | Peak best: after midnight August 12 into pre-dawn August 13, 2026 | Debris from Comet Swift–Tuttle burns in the atmosphere as fast meteors, and 2026 gives a favorable dark-sky peak. | Bright meteors may sweep from the northeast near Perseus, with many crossing far from the radiant. | Best after midnight into pre-dawn. Lie back under a dark sky, feet toward the northeast. No binoculars; naked eye only. | Among the year’s most loved showers, and especially strong in 2026 because moonlight is minimal. | Future. |
| Geminids | Peak best: night of December 13 into pre-dawn December 14, 2026; forecast maximum favors the Dec 13–14 overnight window | Dust from asteroid 3200 Phaethon produces many bright slow meteors, often among the year’s richest displays. | Many bright, slower meteors appear from Gemini in the east/northeast and can cross the whole sky. | Begin late evening, but stay after midnight for the stronger watch. Face east/northeast or lie flat. Use a dark site and naked eye only. | One of the greatest annual showers, and 2026 remains favorable despite some moonlight. | Future. Corrected strongest local window: Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14. |
| Quadrantids | Peak best: after midnight January 3 into pre-dawn January 4, 2027; forecast maximum occurs the evening of Jan 3 in U.S. time zones, but radiant improves before dawn Jan 4 | Debris from asteroid 2003 EH1 meets Earth at a steep angle, producing a brief but sharp shower peak. | The shower is brief and sharp. Meteors appear from the northern sky and are best when the radiant is higher before dawn. | Watch after midnight into pre-dawn. Face north to northeast, lie back, and keep a wide field of view. Dark sky matters more than equipment. | A short, often intense opening marker for the year, though easy to miss if one keeps poor watch. | Future. Added for extension through Mar 20, 2027. |

Extra Meteor Tip: Rates quoted are ZHR (ideal dark-sky, radiant overhead). Real CT-area counts will be lower unless you escape city lights — but even 20–30/hr feels like a sky full of fireworks.

These meteor showers turn the night sky into a natural light show and fit perfectly into the Kemetic tradition of constant sky observation. 2026 is strong overall, especially the moon-free Perseids and low-moon Geminids, with the Quadrantids opening 2027 strongly.

## Planetary Highlights – “Wandering Stars” the Ancients Followed

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Central Time (CT)

All data is confirmed against NASA/JPL, EarthSky, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels, In-The-Sky, Sky & Telescope-style observational guidance, and major public astronomy calendars for U.S. Central Time accuracy.

Universal Planetary Tips (apply to every event):
* Naked eye for the brightest planets (they outshine stars); binoculars or a small telescope reveal moons, rings, or cloud bands.
* Best in dark skies: Use rural horizons far from city light domes — examples include Big Bend/West Texas, Ozarks dark-sky areas, Great Plains sites, lake-country rural parks, or any open dark site away from major metros.
* Track nightly motion — planets “wander” against the fixed stars over days/weeks (the reason ancient Egyptians called them “wandering stars”). Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to CT) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked planets as gods (e.g., Jupiter as Horus, Venus as a form of Isis/Hathor) for omens, temple alignments, and calendar syncing. They recorded positions on papyri and used them alongside Sirius and the Moon for festival timing.

For a Kemetic-style sky journal, track not just one-night events but the nightly drift of each planet against the fixed stars.

These planetary events were the “wandering stars” that guided ancient sky-watchers. The 2026–early 2027 span gives you a strong Venus/Jupiter evening pairing, a corrected Saturn opposition, and a powerful early-2027 run of Venus, Jupiter, and Mars.

7. **Geminids timing:** corrected the strongest local window to Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14.

# 2026–March 20, 2027 Sky Events Grid for U.S. Eastern Time (ET)

Ultra-Detailed User Guide – Expanded for maximum depth. All times are converted for the U.S. Eastern Time Zone unless marked as global/non-visible. Use EDT/EST automatically: U.S. DST started March 8, 2026, ended November 1, 2026, and starts again March 14, 2027. For rise/set, horizon altitude, and eclipse visibility, confirm your exact city in Stellarium/SkySafari/timeanddate because a timezone is not a single observing location. Data cross-checked against NASA, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels/Fred Espenak, American Meteor Society, EarthSky, In-The-Sky, and major astronomy references.

Universal Tips (apply to every event):
* Download Stellarium or SkySafari app → set your exact city/location for real-time rise/set + horizon view.
* Dark-sky spots: Use local dark-sky parks and rural horizons — examples include Cherry Springs, the Adirondacks/Catskills, Shenandoah/Blue Ridge areas, Maine coastal dark-sky areas, or any rural site away from major city light domes.
* Naked eye for 90% of these; binoculars add magic for planets/moon details.
* Safety: Eclipse glasses ONLY for solar events.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked these for the 365-day calendar, Nile floods, and festivals tied to Sirius, equinox balance, and lunar cycles.

**Timezone accuracy note:** Exact astronomical instants are converted to ET. Visibility, rise/set, altitude, and horizon direction still depend on your exact city, especially for eclipses and low-horizon planets.

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Eastern Time (ET)

Universal Lunar Tips (apply to every event):
* Full moons rise near sunset and stay visible all night — easy naked-eye tracking.
* Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to ET) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Dark skies best (Joshua Tree or Anza-Borrego for max brightness/color). Binoculars reveal subtle red tones in eclipses or surface details.
* No equipment needed for any of these — just clear eastern/western horizon.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians used full moons and eclipses for festival timing, ritual offerings, and tracking seasonal Nile cycles (lunar calendar synced with solar/Sirius events).

## Meteor Showers – Naked-Eye Fireworks from Comet Dust

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Eastern Time (ET)

Here’s the exact same depth and style as the Solar and Lunar grids — every entry packed with precise ET viewing windows, scientific breakdowns, what you’ll actually see in the sky, practical U.S.-timezone tips, rarity/significance, Kemetic ties, and notes. Meteor timing is inherently less “to-the-minute” than eclipses or phases; peaks are forecasts and the best actual viewing window is usually the darkest pre-dawn period nearest the peak.

Universal Meteor Tips (apply to every event):
* Best after midnight when the radiant (origin point) is highest — rates double or triple by pre-dawn.
* Lie flat on a reclining chair or blanket, face the radiant direction, and let your eyes dark-adapt for 20–30 minutes (no phone screens or white flashlights — use red light only).
* Dark skies are everything in ET: light pollution cuts rates dramatically. Drive 45–90 minutes to Joshua Tree, Anza-Borrego, or high desert spots for 5–10× more meteors. Binoculars optional for faint trains.
* Track your personal “hourly rate” by counting every 15 minutes — fun way to compare to the predicted zenithal hourly rate (ZHR).
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians were master sky observers but focused more on fixed stars/planets than fleeting meteors. Falling stars were sometimes interpreted as divine messages or “tears of the gods,” adding to their ritual sky-watching tradition alongside Sirius and lunar cycles.

| Event | Exact ET Date & Time | Scientific Breakdown | What You'll See & How to Track | Best Viewing Practices for ET | Rarity / Significance / Kemetic Tie | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lyrids | Peak best: after midnight April 21 into pre-dawn April 22, 2026; forecast maximum around daytime Apr 22 ET, so night viewing is key | Dust left by Comet Thatcher meets Earth’s path, producing swift meteors with occasional bright fireballs. | Short, fast streaks may appear from the northeast near Lyra, but meteors can cross any part of the sky. | Go out after midnight and stay until pre-dawn if possible. Lie back with your feet roughly northeast. Use naked eye only; let your eyes dark-adapt. | An annual shower of moderate strength; notable for occasional fireballs and clean spring observing. | Future. |
| Eta Aquariids | Peak best: pre-dawn May 5, 2026, with May 4 and May 6 also useful; forecast maximum occurs before the radiant is well placed for most U.S. observers | Dust from Halley’s Comet enters Earth’s atmosphere as very fast meteors, often with persistent trains. | Swift meteors rise from the southeast near Eta Aquarii, low in the sky for U.S. observers. They may leave brief glowing trains. | Go out in the final pre-dawn hours, ideally after 3 AM. Face southeast but keep your gaze wide. Avoid phones and city light; the Moon will reduce counts. | A Halley-linked shower always carries weight. In a Kemetic frame it suits broader comet and omen tracking. | Future. Corrected: the Moon is waning gibbous, not waxing gibbous, so reduced counts are expected. |
| Perseids | Peak best: after midnight August 12 into pre-dawn August 13, 2026 | Debris from Comet Swift–Tuttle burns in the atmosphere as fast meteors, and 2026 gives a favorable dark-sky peak. | Bright meteors may sweep from the northeast near Perseus, with many crossing far from the radiant. | Best after midnight into pre-dawn. Lie back under a dark sky, feet toward the northeast. No binoculars; naked eye only. | Among the year’s most loved showers, and especially strong in 2026 because moonlight is minimal. | Future. |
| Geminids | Peak best: night of December 13 into pre-dawn December 14, 2026; forecast maximum favors the Dec 13–14 overnight window | Dust from asteroid 3200 Phaethon produces many bright slow meteors, often among the year’s richest displays. | Many bright, slower meteors appear from Gemini in the east/northeast and can cross the whole sky. | Begin late evening, but stay after midnight for the stronger watch. Face east/northeast or lie flat. Use a dark site and naked eye only. | One of the greatest annual showers, and 2026 remains favorable despite some moonlight. | Future. Corrected strongest local window: Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14. |
| Quadrantids | Peak best: after midnight January 3 into pre-dawn January 4, 2027; forecast maximum occurs the evening of Jan 3 in U.S. time zones, but radiant improves before dawn Jan 4 | Debris from asteroid 2003 EH1 meets Earth at a steep angle, producing a brief but sharp shower peak. | The shower is brief and sharp. Meteors appear from the northern sky and are best when the radiant is higher before dawn. | Watch after midnight into pre-dawn. Face north to northeast, lie back, and keep a wide field of view. Dark sky matters more than equipment. | A short, often intense opening marker for the year, though easy to miss if one keeps poor watch. | Future. Added for extension through Mar 20, 2027. |

Extra Meteor Tip: Rates quoted are ZHR (ideal dark-sky, radiant overhead). Real ET-area counts will be lower unless you escape city lights — but even 20–30/hr feels like a sky full of fireworks.

These meteor showers turn the night sky into a natural light show and fit perfectly into the Kemetic tradition of constant sky observation. 2026 is strong overall, especially the moon-free Perseids and low-moon Geminids, with the Quadrantids opening 2027 strongly.

## Planetary Highlights – “Wandering Stars” the Ancients Followed

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Eastern Time (ET)

Universal Planetary Tips (apply to every event):
* Naked eye for the brightest planets (they outshine stars); binoculars or a small telescope reveal moons, rings, or cloud bands.
* Best in dark skies: Use local dark-sky parks and rural horizons — examples include Cherry Springs, the Adirondacks/Catskills, Shenandoah/Blue Ridge areas, Maine coastal dark-sky areas, or any rural site away from major city light domes.
* Track nightly motion — planets “wander” against the fixed stars over days/weeks (the reason ancient Egyptians called them “wandering stars”). Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to ET) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked planets as gods (e.g., Jupiter as Horus, Venus as a form of Isis/Hathor) for omens, temple alignments, and calendar syncing. They recorded positions on papyri and used them alongside Sirius and the Moon for festival timing.

These planetary events were the “wandering stars” that guided ancient sky-watchers. The 2026–early 2027 span gives you a strong Venus/Jupiter evening pairing, a corrected Saturn opposition, and a powerful early-2027 run of Venus, Jupiter, and Mars.

* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians used full moons and eclipses for festival timing, ritual offerings, and tracking seasonal Nile cycles (lunar calendar synced with solar/Sirius events).

## Meteor Showers – Naked-Eye Fireworks from Comet Dust

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Mountain Time (MT)

Here’s the exact same depth and style as the Solar and Lunar grids — every entry packed with precise MT viewing windows, scientific breakdowns, what you’ll actually see in the sky, practical U.S.-timezone tips, rarity/significance, Kemetic ties, and notes. Meteor timing is inherently less “to-the-minute” than eclipses or phases; peaks are forecasts and the best actual viewing window is usually the darkest pre-dawn period nearest the peak.

Universal Meteor Tips (apply to every event):
* Best after midnight when the radiant (origin point) is highest — rates double or triple by pre-dawn.
* Lie flat on a reclining chair or blanket, face the radiant direction, and let your eyes dark-adapt for 20–30 minutes (no phone screens or white flashlights — use red light only).
* Dark skies are everything in MT: light pollution cuts rates dramatically. Drive 45–90 minutes to Joshua Tree, Anza-Borrego, or high desert spots for 5–10× more meteors. Binoculars optional for faint trains.
* Track your personal “hourly rate” by counting every 15 minutes — fun way to compare to the predicted zenithal hourly rate (ZHR).
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians were master sky observers but focused more on fixed stars/planets than fleeting meteors. Falling stars were sometimes interpreted as divine messages or “tears of the gods,” adding to their ritual sky-watching tradition alongside Sirius and lunar cycles.

| Event | Exact MT Date & Time | Scientific Breakdown | What You'll See & How to Track | Best Viewing Practices for MT | Rarity / Significance / Kemetic Tie | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lyrids | Peak best: after midnight April 21 into pre-dawn April 22, 2026; forecast maximum around daytime Apr 22 MT, so night viewing is key | Dust left by Comet Thatcher meets Earth’s path, producing swift meteors with occasional bright fireballs. | Short, fast streaks may appear from the northeast near Lyra, but meteors can cross any part of the sky. | Go out after midnight and stay until pre-dawn if possible. Lie back with your feet roughly northeast. Use naked eye only; let your eyes dark-adapt. | An annual shower of moderate strength; notable for occasional fireballs and clean spring observing. | Future. |
| Eta Aquariids | Peak best: pre-dawn May 5, 2026, with May 4 and May 6 also useful; forecast maximum occurs before the radiant is well placed for most U.S. observers | Dust from Halley’s Comet enters Earth’s atmosphere as very fast meteors, often with persistent trains. | Swift meteors rise from the southeast near Eta Aquarii, low in the sky for U.S. observers. They may leave brief glowing trains. | Go out in the final pre-dawn hours, ideally after 3 AM. Face southeast but keep your gaze wide. Avoid phones and city light; the Moon will reduce counts. | A Halley-linked shower always carries weight. In a Kemetic frame it suits broader comet and omen tracking. | Future. Corrected: the Moon is waning gibbous, not waxing gibbous, so reduced counts are expected. |
| Perseids | Peak best: after midnight August 12 into pre-dawn August 13, 2026 | Debris from Comet Swift–Tuttle burns in the atmosphere as fast meteors, and 2026 gives a favorable dark-sky peak. | Bright meteors may sweep from the northeast near Perseus, with many crossing far from the radiant. | Best after midnight into pre-dawn. Lie back under a dark sky, feet toward the northeast. No binoculars; naked eye only. | Among the year’s most loved showers, and especially strong in 2026 because moonlight is minimal. | Future. |
| Geminids | Peak best: night of December 13 into pre-dawn December 14, 2026; forecast maximum favors the Dec 13–14 overnight window | Dust from asteroid 3200 Phaethon produces many bright slow meteors, often among the year’s richest displays. | Many bright, slower meteors appear from Gemini in the east/northeast and can cross the whole sky. | Begin late evening, but stay after midnight for the stronger watch. Face east/northeast or lie flat. Use a dark site and naked eye only. | One of the greatest annual showers, and 2026 remains favorable despite some moonlight. | Future. Corrected strongest local window: Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14. |
| Quadrantids | Peak best: after midnight January 3 into pre-dawn January 4, 2027; forecast maximum occurs the evening of Jan 3 in U.S. time zones, but radiant improves before dawn Jan 4 | Debris from asteroid 2003 EH1 meets Earth at a steep angle, producing a brief but sharp shower peak. | The shower is brief and sharp. Meteors appear from the northern sky and are best when the radiant is higher before dawn. | Watch after midnight into pre-dawn. Face north to northeast, lie back, and keep a wide field of view. Dark sky matters more than equipment. | A short, often intense opening marker for the year, though easy to miss if one keeps poor watch. | Future. Added for extension through Mar 20, 2027. |

Extra Meteor Tip: Rates quoted are ZHR (ideal dark-sky, radiant overhead). Real MT-area counts will be lower unless you escape city lights — but even 20–30/hr feels like a sky full of fireworks.

These meteor showers turn the night sky into a natural light show and fit perfectly into the Kemetic tradition of constant sky observation. 2026 is strong overall, especially the moon-free Perseids and low-moon Geminids, with the Quadrantids opening 2027 strongly.

## Planetary Highlights – “Wandering Stars” the Ancients Followed

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Mountain Time (MT)

All data is confirmed against NASA/JPL, EarthSky, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels, In-The-Sky, Sky & Telescope-style observational guidance, and major public astronomy calendars for U.S. Mountain Time accuracy.

Universal Planetary Tips (apply to every event):
* Naked eye for the brightest planets (they outshine stars); binoculars or a small telescope reveal moons, rings, or cloud bands.
* Best in dark skies: Use high desert and mountain dark-sky areas — examples include rural Utah, Colorado plateau sites, Wyoming/Montana open sky, northern Arizona, New Mexico, and other high-elevation locations away from city light.
* Track nightly motion — planets “wander” against the fixed stars over days/weeks (the reason ancient Egyptians called them “wandering stars”). Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to MT) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked planets as gods (e.g., Jupiter as Horus, Venus as a form of Isis/Hathor) for omens, temple alignments, and calendar syncing. They recorded positions on papyri and used them alongside Sirius and the Moon for festival timing.

These planetary events were the “wandering stars” that guided ancient sky-watchers. The 2026–early 2027 span gives you a strong Venus/Jupiter evening pairing, a corrected Saturn opposition, and a powerful early-2027 run of Venus, Jupiter, and Mars.

* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians used full moons and eclipses for festival timing, ritual offerings, and tracking seasonal Nile cycles (lunar calendar synced with solar/Sirius events).

## Meteor Showers – Naked-Eye Fireworks from Comet Dust

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Pacific Time (PT)

Here’s the exact same depth and style as the Solar and Lunar grids — every entry packed with precise PT viewing windows, scientific breakdowns, what you’ll actually see in the sky, practical U.S.-timezone tips, rarity/significance, Kemetic ties, and notes. Meteor timing is inherently less “to-the-minute” than eclipses or phases; peaks are forecasts and the best actual viewing window is usually the darkest pre-dawn period nearest the peak.

Universal Meteor Tips (apply to every event):
* Best after midnight when the radiant (origin point) is highest — rates double or triple by pre-dawn.
* Lie flat on a reclining chair or blanket, face the radiant direction, and let your eyes dark-adapt for 20–30 minutes (no phone screens or white flashlights — use red light only).
* Dark skies are everything in PT: light pollution cuts rates dramatically. Drive 45–90 minutes to Joshua Tree, Anza-Borrego, or high desert spots for 5–10× more meteors. Binoculars optional for faint trains.
* Track your personal “hourly rate” by counting every 15 minutes — fun way to compare to the predicted zenithal hourly rate (ZHR).
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians were master sky observers but focused more on fixed stars/planets than fleeting meteors. Falling stars were sometimes interpreted as divine messages or “tears of the gods,” adding to their ritual sky-watching tradition alongside Sirius and lunar cycles.

| Event | Exact PT Date & Time | Scientific Breakdown | What You'll See & How to Track | Best Viewing Practices for PT | Rarity / Significance / Kemetic Tie | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| Lyrids | Peak best: after midnight April 21 into pre-dawn April 22, 2026; forecast maximum around daytime Apr 22 PT, so night viewing is key | Dust left by Comet Thatcher meets Earth’s path, producing swift meteors with occasional bright fireballs. | Short, fast streaks may appear from the northeast near Lyra, but meteors can cross any part of the sky. | Go out after midnight and stay until pre-dawn if possible. Lie back with your feet roughly northeast. Use naked eye only; let your eyes dark-adapt. | An annual shower of moderate strength; notable for occasional fireballs and clean spring observing. | Future. |
| Eta Aquariids | Peak best: pre-dawn May 5, 2026, with May 4 and May 6 also useful; forecast maximum occurs before the radiant is well placed for most U.S. observers | Dust from Halley’s Comet enters Earth’s atmosphere as very fast meteors, often with persistent trains. | Swift meteors rise from the southeast near Eta Aquarii, low in the sky for U.S. observers. They may leave brief glowing trains. | Go out in the final pre-dawn hours, ideally after 3 AM. Face southeast but keep your gaze wide. Avoid phones and city light; the Moon will reduce counts. | A Halley-linked shower always carries weight. In a Kemetic frame it suits broader comet and omen tracking. | Future. Corrected: the Moon is waning gibbous, not waxing gibbous, so reduced counts are expected. |
| Perseids | Peak best: after midnight August 12 into pre-dawn August 13, 2026 | Debris from Comet Swift–Tuttle burns in the atmosphere as fast meteors, and 2026 gives a favorable dark-sky peak. | Bright meteors may sweep from the northeast near Perseus, with many crossing far from the radiant. | Best after midnight into pre-dawn. Lie back under a dark sky, feet toward the northeast. No binoculars; naked eye only. | Among the year’s most loved showers, and especially strong in 2026 because moonlight is minimal. | Future. |
| Geminids | Peak best: night of December 13 into pre-dawn December 14, 2026; forecast maximum favors the Dec 13–14 overnight window | Dust from asteroid 3200 Phaethon produces many bright slow meteors, often among the year’s richest displays. | Many bright, slower meteors appear from Gemini in the east/northeast and can cross the whole sky. | Begin late evening, but stay after midnight for the stronger watch. Face east/northeast or lie flat. Use a dark site and naked eye only. | One of the greatest annual showers, and 2026 remains favorable despite some moonlight. | Future. Corrected strongest local window: Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14. |
| Quadrantids | Peak best: after midnight January 3 into pre-dawn January 4, 2027; forecast maximum occurs the evening of Jan 3 in U.S. time zones, but radiant improves before dawn Jan 4 | Debris from asteroid 2003 EH1 meets Earth at a steep angle, producing a brief but sharp shower peak. | The shower is brief and sharp. Meteors appear from the northern sky and are best when the radiant is higher before dawn. | Watch after midnight into pre-dawn. Face north to northeast, lie back, and keep a wide field of view. Dark sky matters more than equipment. | A short, often intense opening marker for the year, though easy to miss if one keeps poor watch. | Future. Added for extension through Mar 20, 2027. |

Extra Meteor Tip: Rates quoted are ZHR (ideal dark-sky, radiant overhead). Real PT-area counts will be lower unless you escape city lights — but even 20–30/hr feels like a sky full of fireworks.

These meteor showers turn the night sky into a natural light show and fit perfectly into the Kemetic tradition of constant sky observation. 2026 is strong overall, especially the moon-free Perseids and low-moon Geminids, with the Quadrantids opening 2027 strongly.

## Planetary Highlights – “Wandering Stars” the Ancients Followed

Ultra-Detailed User Guide for U.S. Pacific Time (PT)

Here’s the exact same depth and style as the Solar, Lunar, and Meteor grids — every entry packed with precise PT times, scientific breakdowns, what you’ll actually see in the sky, practical U.S.-timezone tips, rarity/significance, Kemetic ties, and notes. All data is confirmed against NASA/JPL, EarthSky, timeanddate.com, AstroPixels, In-The-Sky, Sky & Telescope-style observational guidance, and major public astronomy calendars for U.S. Pacific Time accuracy.

Universal Planetary Tips (apply to every event):
* Naked eye for the brightest planets (they outshine stars); binoculars or a small telescope reveal moons, rings, or cloud bands.
* Best in dark skies: Use Joshua Tree, Anza-Borrego, eastern Sierra/high desert, Oregon/Washington dark-sky areas, or any rural site away from coastal city light domes.
* Track nightly motion — planets “wander” against the fixed stars over days/weeks (the reason ancient Egyptians called them “wandering stars”). Use Stellarium or SkySafari (set to PT) for exact rise/set and altitude.
* Kemetic note: Ancient Egyptians tracked planets as gods (e.g., Jupiter as Horus, Venus as a form of Isis/Hathor) for omens, temple alignments, and calendar syncing. They recorded positions on papyri and used them alongside Sirius and the Moon for festival timing.

| Event | Exact PT Date & Time | Scientific Breakdown | What You'll See & How to Track | Best Viewing Practices for PT | Rarity / Significance / Kemetic Tie | Notes |
|---|---:|---|---|---|---|---|
| Jupiter at Opposition | Jan 10, 2026, all night; exact opposition ~12:34 AM PST | Earth stands between the Sun and Jupiter, bringing the planet to opposition, full illumination, and its best yearly brightness. | Jupiter shines as a steady bright point all night. Binoculars can show its four main moons; a small telescope shows bands. | Watch after sunset in the east, near midnight toward the south, and before dawn in the west. Use binoculars or a small telescope if available. | The year’s chief Jupiter viewing. In a Kemetic frame, a Horus-like wandering star stands at strength. | Past event; still useful as a reference opposition marker. |
| 6-Planet Parade (Alignment) | Feb 28, 2026, 30–60 min after local sunset; best in evening twilight (PT) | From Earth the wandering stars appear gathered along the ecliptic. This is a line-of-sight alignment, not a physical clustering in space. | The planets line the ecliptic from the western twilight across the sky. Venus and Jupiter are obvious; the faint outer planets need optical aid. | Step out 30–60 minutes after sunset. Start low in the west for Mercury/Venus/Saturn, then sweep along the ecliptic toward Jupiter. Use binoculars for Uranus/Neptune. | A six-planet gathering is uncommon enough to feel ceremonial. It suits the image of gods assembled in the sky. | Past event. Uranus and Neptune are not reliable naked-eye targets in bright skies. |
| Venus-Jupiter Conjunction | Jun 9, 2026; exact conjunction occurs during daylight U.S. time; best visual ~45–75 min after sunset | Venus and Jupiter pass one another closely in our line of sight, separated by about 1.6°. | Venus and Jupiter stand close together low in the west-northwest after sunset, like two bright lamps. | Watch 45–75 minutes after sunset. Choose a clear west-northwest horizon. Naked eye is enough; binoculars frame both worlds together. | A striking pairing of two dominant wandering stars. The image readily suggests union, accord, and balance. | Future. Corrected: exact conjunction is in daylight U.S. time, though the evening of June 9 gives the best PT viewing. |
| Venus at Greatest Eastern Elongation | Aug 14, 2026, 11:00 PM PDT; best visible evenings around Aug 14–15 | Venus reaches its widest apparent separation east of the Sun and thus reigns high as the evening star. | Venus dominates the western evening sky after sunset and remains easy to find because it is far from the Sun in apparent angle. | Begin in twilight and face west. Naked eye is enough. Track it over several evenings to see its station in the sky. | A major Venus station in the yearly sky cycle, fitting themes of beauty, renewal, guidance, and attraction. | Future. Added because the original chart only mentioned Venus dominance in an extra note. |
| Saturn at Opposition | Oct 4, 2026, all night; exact opposition ~5:00 AM PDT | Earth stands between the Sun and Saturn, bringing Saturn to opposition and its best yearly visibility. The rings are lit, though the 2026 tilt is shallow. | Saturn is visible all night as a steady golden point. Its rings require a telescope and appear slimmer than in wide-open ring years. | Watch after sunset in the east, near midnight toward the south, and before dawn in the west. Use a small telescope for rings; binoculars may show Titan under good conditions. | The annual best view of Saturn, a slow and weighty marker suited to endurance, structure, and long measure. | Future. Major correction: the rings are not wide open; 2026 is a shallow post-ring-plane-crossing season. |
| Venus at Greatest Western Elongation | Jan 3, 2027, 11:00 AM PST; best visible before sunrise around Jan 3 | Venus reaches a wide separation west of the Sun and becomes the commanding morning star. | Venus becomes the morning star, bright in the eastern sky before sunrise. | Go out 60–90 minutes before sunrise and face east/southeast. Naked eye is enough; a clear horizon matters. | A strong Venus marker of dawn, renewal, and emergence. | Future. Added for extension. |
| Jupiter at Opposition | Feb 10, 2027, all night; exact opposition ~4:00 PM PST | Earth again stands between the Sun and Jupiter, restoring Jupiter to opposition and full yearly brilliance for the 2027 season. | Jupiter rises near sunset and remains visible through the night as one of the brightest steady lights. | Watch after sunset in the east, near midnight high in the south, and before dawn in the west. Binoculars reveal the Galilean moons. | The annual return of Jupiter’s strength makes a sound fixed marker among the wandering stars. | Future. Added for extension. |
| Mars at Opposition | Feb 19, 2027, all night; exact opposition ~7:30 AM PST | Earth passes between the Sun and Mars, bringing Mars to opposition and its brightest showing of this cycle. | Mars is the reddish-orange light visible for much of the night, brightest around opposition. | Watch near midnight when Mars is highest and steadier. Naked eye shows color and motion; a telescope may show a small disk. | Mars oppositions arrive roughly every 26 months and mark a stronger season for the red planet—heat, force, and disciplined power. | Future. Added for extension. |
| Mercury at Greatest Western Elongation | Mar 16–17, 2027; best visible before sunrise mid-March (PT) | Mercury reaches a favorable western elongation, gaining one of its better morning windows away from the Sun. | Mercury appears low in the eastern dawn and vanishes quickly into sunrise light. | Look 30–45 minutes before sunrise with a clean eastern horizon. Binoculars help, but stop using them before the Sun rises. | Mercury is elusive and rewards the careful observer. Its brief appearance suits the swift messenger among the wandering stars. | Future. Added for extension before Mar 20, 2027. |

Extra Planetary Tip: Mercury has short visibility windows, Venus dominates by morning/evening cycles, Jupiter and Saturn anchor yearly opposition tracking, and Mars becomes a major early-2027 highlight. For a Kemetic-style sky journal, track not just one-night events but the nightly drift of each planet against the fixed stars.

These planetary events were the “wandering stars” that guided ancient sky-watchers. The 2026–early 2027 span gives you a strong Venus/Jupiter evening pairing, a corrected Saturn opposition, and a powerful early-2027 run of Venus, Jupiter, and Mars.

## Cross-Check Corrections Applied

1. **Aug 12, 2026 total solar eclipse:** corrected from “partial phases possible in PT” to **not visible from the Pacific Time Zone**.
2. **Season times:** Summer Solstice corrected to 1:24 AM PDT; Autumn Equinox corrected to 5:05 PM PDT; March 20, 2027 equinox added at 1:24 PM PDT.
3. **Lunar exact times:** corrected several full moon times, especially Sep 26 Harvest Moon, Nov 24 Beaver Moon, and Dec 23 Cold Supermoon.
4. **October 2026 full moon:** added the missing Hunter’s Moon row.
5. **Aug 27, 2026 lunar eclipse:** corrected “93% in umbra” phrasing; 0.930 is the umbral magnitude, while visual disk coverage is closer to about 96%.
6. **Eta Aquariid Moon wording:** corrected waxing gibbous to waning gibbous.
7. **Geminids timing:** corrected the strongest local window to Dec 13–14, especially after midnight into pre-dawn Dec 14.
8. **Saturn rings:** corrected “wide open ~20°” to shallow 2026 ring tilt conditions.
9. **Venus-Jupiter conjunction:** corrected exact closest timing from evening to daylight; kept the evening of June 9 as the best visible U.S. view.
10. **Extension added:** Feb 6, 2027 solar eclipse non-visible in PT; Jan 22 and Feb 20, 2027 full moons; Quadrantids; Venus/Jupiter/Mars/Mercury 2027 planetary highlights; Mar 20, 2027 spring equinox.


**Source note:** Built from the corrected Pacific master chart, then converted by U.S. timezone and adapted for non-city-specific visibility. Eclipse visibility remains location-specific.

## 2. Dawn House Rite

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/dawn_house_rite_flow.dart
- Key: dawn-house-rite
- Overview: The Dawn House Rite rests on a clear Kemetic time-pattern: at dawn, the world returns to visible order. This thirty-day flow turns that pattern into a simple house observance: set water, greet the returning light, speak ma’at, and choose one act that brings order into the day. Its three ten-day movements carry dawn renewal from the body to the home to the wider world.

### 1. Opening the Day

- Day: 1
- Section: Personal Ma'at
- Title: Opening the Day
- Purpose: Dawn is the daily proof that order returns. This rite enters that return deliberately.
- Words: The day opens. I enter it in Ma'at. May my hands be clean, my speech be true, and my actions give life.
- Action: Wash hands and face. Set water. Face the light.
- Ma'at act: Name one thing you will set in order today.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
Dawn is the daily proof that order returns. This rite enters that return deliberately.

Action
Wash hands and face. Set water. Face the light.

Ma'at Act
Name one thing you will set in order today.
```

### 2. Purifying the Self

- Day: 2
- Section: Personal Ma'at
- Title: Purifying the Self
- Purpose: You accumulated things overnight — thought residue, yesterday's unfinished business. This rite clears before engaging.
- Words: I am cleansed for the day. May falsehood fall away. May what is useful remain.
- Action: Wash hands slowly. Touch water to lips or rinse the mouth.
- Ma'at act: Avoid one source of disorder today: gossip, haste, resentment, neglect, or waste.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
You accumulated things overnight — thought residue, yesterday's unfinished business. This rite clears before engaging.

Action
Wash hands slowly. Touch water to lips or rinse the mouth.

Ma'at Act
Avoid one source of disorder today: gossip, haste, resentment, neglect, or waste.
```

### 3. Right Speech

- Day: 3
- Section: Personal Ma'at
- Title: Right Speech
- Purpose: What you say first shapes how you say everything after. This rite makes the mouth's first act intentional.
- Words: May my mouth speak Ma'at. May I not multiply confusion. May my words steady what they touch.
- Action: Wash or touch the mouth with water. Pause before messages or conversation.
- Ma'at act: Speak one necessary truth kindly, or keep silence where speech would harm.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

What you say first shapes how you say everything after. This rite makes the mouth's first act intentional.

```

- Day: 4
- Section: Personal Ma'at
- Title: Clear Sight
- Purpose: Sleep clouds more than the eyes. This rite restores sight before you're asked to see clearly.
- Words: The light returns. What was hidden becomes visible. May I see clearly and act in right measure.
- Action: Wash face, especially brow or eyes. Look toward the light.
- Ma'at act: Name today's true priority before beginning lesser tasks.

- Purpose: Every imbalance started as a small drift. This rite sets the proportion before anything has gone too far.
- Words: May I move in right measure. May I neither rush nor neglect. May my strength be used where it belongs.
- Action: Set water. Take three measured breaths before speaking.
- Ma'at act: Choose one limit: time, food, spending, speech, work, or emotional reaction.

## 3. Evening Threshold Rite

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/evening_threshold_rite_flow.dart
- Key: evening-threshold-rite
- Overview: The Closing is a thirty-day evening flow rooted in the Kemetic pattern of sunset as passage. As visible action gives way to hidden renewal, the user closes one open loop, settles one small disorder, offers gratitude or water, dims one light, and speaks a short line of release. Across three ten-day movements, the rite turns evening into a daily act of Ma’at: finish what can be finished, release what must wait, and prepare the self and home for restoration.

### 1. The Threshold

- Day: 1
- Section: Closing the Visible Day
- Title: The Threshold
- Purpose: The evening has no official start time. This rite marks one deliberately.
- Words: The visible day closes. I step across the threshold in Ma'at.
- Action: Pause near a window, doorway, lamp, or sink. Notice that the visible day has ended.
- Evening act: Close one open loop: reply, schedule, write down, put away, or release until tomorrow.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The evening has no official start time. This rite marks one deliberately.

Action
Pause near a window, doorway, lamp, or sink. Notice that the visible day has ended.

Evening Act
Close one open loop: reply, schedule, write down, put away, or release until tomorrow.
```

- Title: Extinguishing Excess

- Action: Dim one light, silence one alert, or step away from one screen.
- Evening act: Remove one source of overstimulation.

#### Canonical Detail Text


- Action: Set a cup or bowl of water, or wash your hands at the sink.
- Evening act: Drink water, wash hands, water a plant, or leave water briefly as an offering.

## 4. The Weighing

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_weighing_flow.dart
- Key: the-weighing
- Tagline: Sit with what is true.
- Overview: The Weighing is a thirty-day Ma’at reckoning flow with nine sittings across material records, spoken records, and conduct. It asks the user to place one real thing on the scale, name one gap, and choose one correction without turning the practice into shame, confession, or self-punishment.

### 1. Open the Material Ledger

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Material Ledger
- Title: Open the Material Ledger
- Words: I open my record without fear. What is here is what is true.
- Steps:
  1. Place a cup of water on your surface before you begin.
  2. Keep the water there while you write.
  3. Speak the line before opening any record.
  4. Write one number you have not looked at directly in the last ten days: a balance, debt, supply, or physical record.
  5. Name one thing you received in the last ten days by saying plainly: I received ___.
- Source note: The Kemite placed water first because the record must be witnessed before it can be weighed — not examined, not solved, but witnessed. What sustains you is named before anything else is counted.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I open my record without fear. What is here is what is true."

Steps
1. Place a cup of water on your surface before you begin.
2. Keep the water there while you write.
3. Speak the line before opening any record.
4. Write one number you have not looked at directly in the last ten days: a balance, debt, supply, or physical record.
5. Name one thing you received in the last ten days by saying plainly: I received ___.
```

### 2. Check the Material Scale

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Material Ledger
- Title: Check the Material Scale
- Words: The measure is the eye of Re. I do not adjust it to favor myself.
- Steps:
  1. Name one place in the last five days where the actual count was softened.
  2. Choose one small restorative act before Day 10 closes if the imbalance is correctable.
- Source note: Amenemope's Chapter 17 is specific: the grain measure belongs to Re, and tampering with it is theft from the god, not just from the transaction. The midpoint check is the moment the scale speaks again, without interference.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Choose one small restorative act before Day 10 closes if the imbalance is correctable.
```

### 3. Seal the Material Record

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Material Ledger
- Title: Seal the Material Record
- Words: I seal this period with what is true. I have not added to the weight of the balance. I have not subtracted from what is owed.
- Steps:
  1. Complete one financial, nutritional, physical, or domestic record that has been left open.
  2. Write one closing line naming the actual state of this thing.
  3. Name one resource you will handle with more accuracy in the next ten-day section.
- Optional steps:
  1. Extinguish a light when you complete the event.
- Source note: The Declaration of Innocence names false balance and tampered scales in the same breath as murder and theft. The Kemite treated the distorted record as harm to the world, not as an inconvenience to the individual.

### 4. Open the Spoken Record

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Spoken Record
- Title: Open the Spoken Record
- Words: My tongue is the plummet. My heart is the weight. I do not utter falsehood, for I am a balance.
- Steps:
  1. Place water on your surface before sitting.
  2. Speak the line before writing anything.
  3. Name one promise or agreement that remains open from the last ten days.
  4. State what was said.
  5. State what has been done.
  6. State what remains undone.
  7. Name one account you softened, exaggerated, or framed to favor yourself.
- Source note: The Eloquent Peasant calls the honest person a balance whose tongue, heart, and lips carry the measure. A balance adjusted for convenience is no longer a balance — it is a prop. This sitting asks whether the spoken record has been a real balance.

#### Canonical Detail Text

### 5. Check the Spoken Scale

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Spoken Record
- Title: Check the Spoken Scale
- Words: Do not lead a man astray with reed pen on papyrus. It is the abomination of God.
- Steps:
  1. Name one person whose understanding of an agreement with you may differ from yours.
  2. Write one sentence about the difference.
  3. Only if the gap can close with one clear message, send it before this ten-day section ends.
- Source note: Amenemope treats written false witness as a grave disorder — not because writing is sacred, but because what is written persists and shapes what follows. The spoken and written record do the same damage when they drift from what was actually agreed.

### 6. Seal the Spoken Record

- Event number: 6
- Flow day: 19
- Section: Spoken Record
- Title: Seal the Spoken Record
- Words: I have not slandered a servant to his superior. I have not caused weeping. I seal my spoken record with what I know to be true.
- Steps:
  1. Name one spoken obligation from the second ten days that was fulfilled.
  2. Say it fully: I said I would ___, and I did ___.
  3. Name one spoken obligation that remains open.
  4. State that this obligation is being carried into the final ten days.
- Optional steps:
  1. Write the one thing you are carrying forward on paper, not a screen.
- Source note: Spell 125 names slander and causing unnecessary pain alongside larger violations because Kemetic moral accounting treated them as the same category: disorder introduced into another person's life. The seal closes honestly about what the spoken record actually produced.

### 7. Open the Record You Leave

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Record You Leave
- Title: Open the Record You Leave
- Words: I have come before you, my lord, bringing Truth, having repelled for you falsehood.
- Steps:
  1. Stand before reading the four lines.
  2. Speak the line before reading the four lines.
  3. Read slowly: I have not caused avoidable hunger in those who depend on me.
  4. Read slowly: I have not diverted what should have flowed.
  5. Read slowly: I have not made my record better than my conduct.
  6. Read slowly: I have not taken more than is due.
  7. Write one corrective act you can complete before this final ten-day section closes if any line required a pause.
- Source note: The Declaration of Innocence was spoken before the 42 Assessors as a formal account of conduct, not a confession. Each line was weighed on its own. A pause at any line is information, not judgment — it shows where the record needs attention before it is sealed.

```text
4. Read slowly: I have not diverted what should have flowed.
5. Read slowly: I have not made my record better than my conduct.
6. Read slowly: I have not taken more than is due.
7. Write one corrective act you can complete before this final ten-day section closes if any line required a pause.
```

### 8. Check the Living Record

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: Record You Leave
- Title: Check the Living Record
- Words: Do not go off course, for you are impartiality. If the balance wavers, then you will waver.
- Steps:
  1. Ask whether someone with less power in a relationship would give a significantly different account of the last three weeks.
  2. Sit with that possibility for one minute without arguing against it.
  3. Write one sentence about what you think they would say.
- Source note: The Eloquent Peasant says impartiality is a condition of truthful speech — the judge who favors one side cannot call themselves a balance. This midpoint check asks whether the living record has applied the same measure to those with less and those with more.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

3. Write one sentence about what you think they would say.
```

### 9. Seal the Record

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Record You Leave
- Title: Seal the Record
- Words: I am pure. I am pure. I am pure. I am pure.
- Steps:
  1. Speak only the truth-check lines you can speak honestly.
  2. Remain silent on any truth-check line that is not accurate.
  3. Say, if true: The material record of this ten-day section was kept without distortion.
  4. Say, if true: The spoken record of this ten-day section was kept without distortion.
  5. Say, if true: What I carried forward, I carried forward clearly.
  6. Say, if true: I did not take more than was due.
  7. Say, if true: I did not leave unprovided those in my care.
  8. Say, if true: I did not adjust the scale to favor myself.
  9. Speak the closing declaration after the truth-check lines.
  10. Name one thing from this cycle that you will hold differently in the next.
  11. Name one thing the record shows you did well.
  12. Place a cup of water on your surface.
  13. Let the water stand as the final offering.
- Source note: The Weighing of the Heart did not require perfection — it required honesty. The heart heavier or lighter than the feather had been inaccurate, not evil. The seal closes the record so continuation can begin from truth rather than pretense.

## 5. The Offering Table

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_offering_table_flow.dart
- Key: the-offering-table
- Tagline: Feed what needs to be fed.
- Overview: The Offering Table is a thirty-day provision flow. Each morning begins with water, then moves through food, rest, care, household support, and what must be returned to flow. The practice stays small: feed what needs to be fed before the day takes everything else.
- Enrollment copy: Required: place water and speak the line. Everything else is optional. Two minutes is enough.

### 1. The First Water

- Day: 1
- Section: Personal Table
- Title: The First Water
- Purpose: The Kemetic offering table began with water before anything else — not because water is symbolic, but because it is the most immediate provision. This rite does the same.
- Provision act: Before food, phone, or work, fill the cup. Name one basic need that has been unmet for three days or more.
- Optional steps:
  1. Write the need in one sentence without explaining it away.
- Source note: Kemetic offering ritual begins with water before bread, oil, or incense. The table starts by acknowledging what sustains life first.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The Kemetic offering table began with water before anything else — not because water is symbolic, but because it is the most immediate provision. This rite does the same.

Water
Place a cup of water before food, phone, or work.

Words
"Wash yourself and your Ka will wash itself. Your Ka will sit and eat bread with you without ceasing."

Provision
Before food, phone, or work, fill the cup. Name one basic need that has been unmet for three days or more.

Optional
- Write the need in one sentence without explaining it away.

Drink
Drink the water. This is reversion: provision returns through the living body, not left on the table.
```

### 2. The Cup Before the Noise

- Day: 2
- Section: Personal Table
- Title: The Cup Before the Noise
- Purpose: The day's demands begin competing for attention before the body has been given anything. This rite reverses that order.
- Provision act: Drink water before opening any feed, message thread, or task list. Name what you want your first input to be today.
- Optional steps:
  1. Move the phone or laptop one arm-length away while you drink.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Provision

Optional
- Move the phone or laptop one arm-length away while you drink.

Drink
Drink the water. This is reversion: provision returns through the living body, not left on the table.
```

### 3. Bread Enough

- Day: 3
- Section: Personal Table
- Title: Bread Enough
- Purpose: Food treated as fuel is not being received — it's being used. This rite makes one meal an act of provision rather than consumption.
- Provision act: Name your first real food for the day. If it is not planned, choose one reachable option before the morning moves on.
- Optional steps:
  1. Place or prepare one food item where you will see it.

- Day: 4
- Section: Personal Table
- Title: The Body Washed
- Purpose: Neglect rarely announces itself — it accumulates in small deferrals. This rite names one and corrects it.
- Provision act: Wash face, hands, or mouth with attention. Name one body-care task that has been delayed and choose its smallest version.
- Optional steps:
  1. Set out the item needed for that care: towel, medicine, lotion, clean clothes, or shoes.

- Provision act: Name last night's sleep hours as a fact. Name one thing likely to shorten sleep tonight, and reduce it by one small amount.
- Optional steps:
  1. Set one evening boundary now: time, screen, food, work, or conversation.
- Source note: Provision is not only food. The Ka is sustained by repeated supports, and rest is one of the supports that disappears when it is not counted.

- Optional steps:
  1. Say: I am allowed to be provisioned without proving exhaustion first.

- Provision act: Name one personal need that was fed this decan and one need still asking for provision. Keep both statements short.
- Optional steps:
  1. Prepare tomorrow's first water before the day closes.

- Optional steps:
  1. Make one small provision act now if it can be done without drama.

### 17. The Care Message

- Title: The Care Message

### 30. The Table Is Complete

- Day: 30
- Section: Flowing Table
- Title: The Table Is Complete
- Purpose: The table was never about perfection — it was about the record being honest. The cycle closes with accuracy, not with achievement.
- Provision act:
  1. Speak only the lines that are true.
  2. Say, if true: My water was placed with attention.
  3. Say, if true: Food, rest, or care was not treated as imaginary.
  4. Say, if true: I fed one need before it became collapse.
  5. Say, if true: I noticed who else depends on the table.
  6. Say, if true: What flowed to me was allowed to return.
  7. Name one shortfall.
  8. Name one provision that surprised you.
- Optional steps:
  1. Sit for one quiet breath after drinking the water.
  2. Share only this prompt if you choose: one thing the table held that I did not expect.
- Source note: The offering table is not an end point. The water is consumed, the support returns to life, and the next cycle starts from what is now known.

## 6. The Tending

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_tending_flow.dart
- Key: the-tending
- Tagline: Find who needs you. Do the labor.
- Overview: The Tending is a thirty-day care flow with nine sittings. It asks the user to look clearly at who or what is in their care, complete one specific tending act, and repair what was missed without turning care into guilt or vague sentiment.

### 1. The First Seeing

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Find and See
- Title: The First Seeing
- Words: I look for who is in my care. I do not let need become invisible.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the line before writing anything.
  2. Name who is in your care in this season: person, animal, household, plant, place, promise, or vulnerable responsibility.
  3. Write one perceived need beside each name. Do not solve the whole list.
  4. Choose one name that should not remain unseen in this ten-day section.
- Optional steps:
  1. If the list feels too large, circle only the name with the nearest real-world need.
- Source note: In the Osirian pattern, Aset and Nebet-Het search first — not because they don't know where Ausar is, but because searching is what begins restoration. Seeing the care field clearly is the same act: what cannot be seen cannot be gathered.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I look for who is in my care. I do not let need become invisible."

Steps
1. Speak the line before writing anything.
2. Name who is in your care in this season: person, animal, household, plant, place, promise, or vulnerable responsibility.
3. Write one perceived need beside each name. Do not solve the whole list.
4. Choose one name that should not remain unseen in this ten-day section.

Optional
- If the list feels too large, circle only the name with the nearest real-world need.
```

### 2. Listen Without Dismissing

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Find and See
- Title: Listen Without Dismissing
- Words: I hear the need before I explain it away.
- Steps:
  1. Name one need expressed by someone in your care during the last week.
  2. Write the exact words, gesture, silence, or behavior that carried the need.
  3. Write the sentence without correcting, defending, or making it about yourself.
- Optional steps:
  1. If direct contact is not possible, observe one concrete condition that tells the truth.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I hear the need before I explain it away."

Steps
1. Name one need expressed by someone in your care during the last week.
2. Write the exact words, gesture, silence, or behavior that carried the need.
3. Write the sentence without correcting, defending, or making it about yourself.

Optional
- If direct contact is not possible, observe one concrete condition that tells the truth.
```

### 3. Seal the Seeing

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Find and See
- Title: Seal the Seeing
- Words: What I have seen, I will not pretend not to know.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the care inventory.
  2. Mark each visible need as tended, partial, or unseen.
  3. Choose one unseen name to carry explicitly into the next ten-day section.
- Optional steps:
  1. Send one brief message if seeing alone would become avoidance.
- Source note: Aset did not begin by restoring Ausar — she began by finding him. What has been found and named is what the second decan can work with.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"What I have seen, I will not pretend not to know."

Steps
1. Return to the care inventory.
2. Mark each visible need as tended, partial, or unseen.
3. Choose one unseen name to carry explicitly into the next ten-day section.

Optional
- Send one brief message if seeing alone would become avoidance.
```

### 4. Begin the Labor

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Gather and Attend
- Title: Begin the Labor
- Words: I gather what care requires. Warmth is not labor until it acts.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one care obligation from the first ten-day section.
  2. Name the specific labor required: call, feed, clean, carry, pay, schedule, sit with, protect, repair, or ask.
  3. Commit to one act that can be completed before Day 19.
- Optional steps:
  1. If you are burned out, choose the smallest honest act that does not deepen harm to you.
- Source note: In the Heru restoration pattern, scattered reality had to be physically gathered before standing was possible. Care that remains an intention is still scattered. This decan gathers it into specific acts.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I gather what care requires. Warmth is not labor until it acts."

Steps
1. Choose one care obligation from the first ten-day section.
2. Name the specific labor required: call, feed, clean, carry, pay, schedule, sit with, protect, repair, or ask.
3. Commit to one act that can be completed before Day 19.

Optional
- If you are burned out, choose the smallest honest act that does not deepen harm to you.
```

### 5. One Act, Done

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Gather and Attend
- Title: One Act, Done
- Words: One act of care completed is better than a feeling left unnamed.
- Steps:
  1. Do one small tending act now.
  2. If you cannot do it now, name the exact time it will happen today.
  3. Record what changed after the act, even if it was very small.
  4. If the act could not be done, name the exact blocker without self-excuse.
- Optional steps:
  1. Ask for help if the tending requires more hands than yours.

### 6. Seal the Tending

- Event number: 6
- Flow day: 19
- Section: Gather and Attend
- Title: Seal the Tending
- Words: I seal this labor by what was done, not by what I meant.
- Steps:
  1. Review the Day 11 commitment.
  2. Name what was tended.
  3. Name what was partial.
  4. Name what was avoided.
  5. Carry only one unfinished care obligation into the final ten-day section.
- Optional steps:
  1. If you owe repair, write the first sentence plainly: I missed ___. I will ___.
- Source note: Care is not sealed by intention. It is sealed by the specific act completed. What was partial is named as partial. What was avoided is named as avoided. The record stays honest so the restoration decan can start clean.

### 7. Begin the Restoration

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Stand and Restore
- Title: Begin the Restoration
- Words: I stand where care was missed. I repair what is mine to repair.
- Steps:
  1. Name one care obligation missed or only partly tended.
  2. Name the harm or strain created without exaggerating it.
  3. Choose one repair act completable before Day 29.
- Optional steps:
  1. If repair requires apology, make it brief and specific. Do not ask the person in your care to manage your guilt.
- Source note: Heru's standing was restoration, not domination — he stood up where the order had collapsed. The repair act goes toward the damage, not away from it.

```text
Optional
- If repair requires apology, make it brief and specific. Do not ask the person in your care to manage your guilt.
```

### 8. The Check on Repair

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: Stand and Restore
- Title: The Check on Repair
- Words: Repair is not declared complete while the need remains untouched.
- Steps:
  1. Check whether the repair act named on Day 21 has actually been attempted.
  2. Write what moved.
  3. Write what did not move.
  4. Write what still belongs to you.
  5. If the repair is blocked by another person, write what remains yours.
  6. Release what is not yours to repair.

### 9. The Tending Is Complete

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Stand and Restore
- Title: The Tending Is Complete
- Words: I have not turned away from those placed in my care.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the line once before writing any closing line.
  2. Check whether the line is true.
  3. For each name you held locally, write one private closing line: tended, partial, unseen, or repaired.
  4. Name one thing the labor of this cycle restored.
  5. Name one tending practice you will carry into the next cycle without making a vow you cannot keep.
- Optional steps:
  1. Name who tended you in this cycle. Care is not only what leaves your hands.
  2. If you share, share only the generic restoration line. Do not share names or care-list details.
- Source note: Aset's work was not finished in one period — it continued across the mythological record. What you close is this cycle's portion, not the whole account.
