## 7. The Kept Word

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_kept_word_flow.dart
- Key: the-kept-word
- Tagline: What you speak within your house determines its order.
- Overview: The Kept Word is a thirty-day agreement flow with nine sittings. It names what was spoken, bent, broken, repaired, or released, then asks for one clearer act of order while keeping another person’s response outside the user’s control.

### 1. The Inventory of Agreements

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Name the State
- Title: The Inventory of Agreements
- Words: Speak Ma'at within your palace, so that those around you may respect you. An upright heart is becoming to a lord; it is the front of a house which creates respect for the back.
- Steps:
  1. Write the two or three people with whom you share the most significant household or working agreements.
  2. For each person, write the current agreements: who handles what, who said what, and what was last promised.
  3. Mark each agreement kept, drifted, or broken.
  4. Mark any drifted or broken agreement for the next ten-day section.
- Source note: Merikare's teaching placed truth inside the palace first because that is where order is most likely to be protected or corrupted. Proximity makes drift comfortable — the closest agreements are the ones where it goes unnoticed longest.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Speak Ma'at within your palace, so that those around you may respect you. An upright heart is becoming to a lord; it is the front of a house which creates respect for the back."

Steps
1. Write the two or three people with whom you share the most significant household or working agreements.
2. For each person, write the current agreements: who handles what, who said what, and what was last promised.
3. Mark each agreement kept, drifted, or broken.
4. Mark any drifted or broken agreement for the next ten-day section.
```

### 2. Name the Shared Rhythm

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Name the State
- Title: Name the Shared Rhythm
- Words: Do not utter falsehood, for you are a balance. Do not go off course, for you are impartiality. If it wavers, then you will waver.
- Steps:
  1. Name one shared rhythm that used to hold the household or relationship together and has drifted or stopped.
  2. Name who was affected by the rhythm stopping.
  3. Write one sentence about whether anyone has named the change directly.
- Source note: The Eloquent Peasant names the person as a balance. This sitting treats shared rhythm as part of the household measure.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Do not utter falsehood, for you are a balance. Do not go off course, for you are impartiality. If it wavers, then you will waver."

Steps
```

## 8. The Course

### 1. The Solar Course: Locate at Dawn

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Daily Course
- Title: The Solar Course: Locate at Dawn
- Words: Riser, Riser! Beetle, Beetle! Your life is related to mine; my life is related to yours. Sustenance is for my morning, Abundance is for my evening. Famine will not have control of this life.
- Steps:
  1. Face east, or face the window.
  2. Open the ḥꜣw day card. Read the Kemetic date, decan name, and Ma'at principle before you close it.
  3. Name one thing appropriate for the morning part of this particular day.
  4. Do one opening act that matches the day instead of only the task list.
- Source note: Pyramid Texts Utterance 388 addressed the returning Sun as an entity that had just arrived and with whom the speaker was in reciprocal relationship. The day card locates you in the Kemetic count; facing east makes the location physical.

  3. Name one thing completed and one thing that now passes into night.
- Source note: The solar course moves from dawn birth through dusk completion and the hidden night passage.

## 9. The Moon Return

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/moon_return_flow.dart
- Key: the-moon-return
- Tagline: The eye empties and fills. Receive what the cycle gives.
- Overview: The Moon Return follows the lunar rhythm of emptying and fullness. At the new moon, the user sets down one thing; at the full moon, they name what has filled. The flow keeps the record short and tied to visible lunar return.
- Confidence: Draws on new/full moon timing in Kemetic sacred calendars and the Eye of Heru cycle.

### The Empty Eye / New Moon

- Title: The Empty Eye / New Moon
- Purpose: The new moon opens an empty-sky threshold: one thing from the last cycle can be set down without needing the moon to be visible.
- Words: The eye has gone. The sky is cleared. I set down what the last cycle carried and let the dark receive it.
- Steps:
  1. Step outside at dusk.
  2. Stand with the empty sky.
  3. Name one thing from the last lunar cycle that you are setting down.
  4. Variant step, Wep Ronpet new: Name the year-opening threshold plainly: what should not cross into the opened year?
  5. Variant step, solar eclipse new: If this is an eclipse window, name the rare darkness as a marked threshold.
  6. Speak the line.
  7. Return inside.
- Outdoor: The new moon event requires going outside at dusk, but it does not require seeing the moon. If safety, access, or weather prevents going outside, stand at a window or threshold and keep the empty-sky witness.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The new moon opens an empty-sky threshold: one thing from the last cycle can be set down without needing the moon to be visible.

Words
"The eye has gone. The sky is cleared. I set down what the last cycle carried and let the dark receive it."

Steps
1. Step outside at dusk.
2. Stand with the empty sky.
3. Name one thing from the last lunar cycle that you are setting down.
4. Variant step, Wep Ronpet new: Name the year-opening threshold plainly: what should not cross into the opened year?
5. Variant step, solar eclipse new: If this is an eclipse window, name the rare darkness as a marked threshold.
6. Speak the line.
7. Return inside.
```

### The Whole Eye / Full Moon

- Title: The Whole Eye / Full Moon
- Purpose: The full moon marks the Eye restored to wholeness; what has filled in this cycle is named under its returning light.
- Words: Horus has filled you complete with his eye. The Eye of Heru is whole. I receive what has been given.
- Steps:
  1. Go outside at moonrise.
  2. If clouds block the moon, face its direction and stand under the sky anyway.
  3. Stand for one minute in the full moon sky.
  4. Variant step, blue moon: Notice that the Eye fills twice this Gregorian month; receive what filled again.
  5. Variant step, lunar eclipse full: If the moon passes through shadow tonight, name the restoration that returns from shadow.
  6. Variant step, supermoon full: Notice the nearest full Eye of the year and receive accordingly.
  7. Name one thing that has filled since the new moon: In this cycle, ___ filled.
  8. Speak the line.
  9. Return inside.
- Outdoor: The full moon event requires going outside at moonrise unless safety, access, or weather prevents it. Clouds are acceptable; presence under the sky is the act.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The full moon marks the Eye restored to wholeness; what has filled in this cycle is named under its returning light.

Words
"Horus has filled you complete with his eye. The Eye of Heru is whole. I receive what has been given."

Steps
1. Go outside at moonrise.
2. If clouds block the moon, face its direction and stand under the sky anyway.
3. Stand for one minute in the full moon sky.
4. Variant step, blue moon: Notice that the Eye fills twice this Gregorian month; receive what filled again.
5. Variant step, lunar eclipse full: If the moon passes through shadow tonight, name the restoration that returns from shadow.
6. Variant step, supermoon full: Notice the nearest full Eye of the year and receive accordingly.
7. Name one thing that has filled since the new moon: In this cycle, ___ filled.
8. Speak the line.
9. Return inside.
```

### Variant Copy

- The new moon is also a solar eclipse. The Eye covers the Sun: this threshold is marked.
- Tonight the Eye passes through shadow and returns. Use the blood moon framing.
- The Eye fills twice this month. This is a bonus Whole Eye for users already in the practice.
- Tonight the Eye is nearest and brighter than ordinary. Receive what has filled.
- The Moon covers the Sun as the Kemetic year opens. Begin here with maximum prominence.

## 10. The Wag

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_wag_flow.dart
- Key: the-wag
- Tagline: Speak their names. Set the table. They are not gone.
- Overview: The Wag is an annual Kemetic ancestor flow in Month 1. It moves through naming, table-setting, vigil, feast, and return, asking the user to carry one gift, memory, or legacy forward without forcing private names into the public record.

### 1. Begin the Naming

- Event number: 1
- Section: The Naming
- Title: Begin the Naming
- Words: Your name shall live at the fore of the living. May your name not perish on earth.
- Steps:
  1. Write the known names on paper: parents, grandparents, great-grandparents, and the dead you personally knew.
  2. For unknown names, write: [Name unknown] - [their relationship to you].
  3. Add one ancestor of practice, craft, community, or way of being.
  4. Read each name aloud once.
  5. Do not read silently for this rite.
- Optional steps:
  1. Use the local list only as a private backup; the handwritten page is the primary act.
- Source note: The Kemite understood the ren — the name — as a constituent part of the person, as real as the body. A name not spoken eventually becomes as if it never existed. This sitting is not commemorative — it is active. Speaking the name continues what the name holds.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Your name shall live at the fore of the living. May your name not perish on earth."

Steps
1. Write the known names on paper: parents, grandparents, great-grandparents, and the dead you personally knew.
2. For unknown names, write: [Name unknown] - [their relationship to you].
3. Add one ancestor of practice, craft, community, or way of being.
4. Read each name aloud once.
5. Do not read silently for this rite.

Optional
- Use the local list only as a private backup; the handwritten page is the primary act.
```

### 2. Extend the Names

- Event number: 2
- Section: The Naming
- Title: Extend the Names
- Words: Your name shall live at the fore of the living.
- Steps:
  1. Look at the Day 1 list and add anyone missing.
  2. Name one ancestor known only by story, or one ancestor of your practice or tradition.
  3. Place water on the surface while you add to the list.
- Source note: Kemetic offering practice acknowledged that not all names were known — the formula could address ancestors by relationship rather than personal name. The gap in the record is not a gap in the obligation. Unknown ancestors are addressed as such, not omitted.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Your name shall live at the fore of the living."

Steps
1. Look at the Day 1 list and add anyone missing.
2. Name one ancestor known only by story, or one ancestor of your practice or tradition.
3. Place water on the surface while you add to the list.
```

### 3. The Table Is Set

- Event number: 3
- Section: The Naming
- Title: The Table Is Set
- Words: I have not neglected the days concerning their meat offerings.
- Steps:
  1. Set water before you read any names.
  2. Read the complete list of names aloud.
  3. After each name, say: I speak your name. You live.
- Source note: Spell 125 lists neglect of the appointed offering days alongside more dramatic wrongs because the Kemite understood that the dead depended on the living's continued attention in a practical sense. The water drunk at the end is the reversion — the provision received by the one who gave it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I have not neglected the days concerning their meat offerings."

Steps
1. Set water before you read any names.
2. Read the complete list of names aloud.
3. After each name, say: I speak your name. You live.
```

### 4. The Approach

- Event number: 4
- Section: The Offering and the Wag
- Title: The Approach
- Words: I have given bread to the hungry, water to the thirsty. I have made invocation-offerings for the blessed dead.
- Steps:
  1. Identify the physical surface where the Wag will be held.
  2. Identify the offerings: water first, then bread or food, and if possible something fragrant.
  3. Write one sentence about who you most want to acknowledge at the Wag.
- Optional steps:
  1. Clear the surface now and leave it empty until the vigil.
- Source note: The prt-ḫrw — the "coming forth at the voice" — was the technical term for the invocation-offering. The provision came into existence through the act of speaking it. Naming who you are speaking for makes the voice's work specific.

### 5. The Vigil

- Event number: 5
- Section: The Offering and the Wag
- Title: The Vigil
- Words: His heart joyful with the calendrical offerings of the processions of every god on the Wag-festival. His heart joyful on the Wag-festival and an eternity of years therein.
- Steps:
  1. Go to the surface prepared on Day 11.
  2. Place water, bread or food, and anything fragrant there now.
  3. Read the complete list of names aloud at dusk.
  4. Do not hurry the reading.
  5. Leave the offerings on the surface through the night.
- Source note: The Neferhotep Stela places joy in the heart of the dead at their calendrical provision — not as metaphor but as the expected state of the blessed dead receiving what was owed. The vigil keeps the table open for that reception.

### 6. The Feast

- Event number: 6
- Section: The Offering and the Wag
- Title: The Feast
- Words: A thousand of bread, a thousand of beer, a thousand of cattle, a thousand of fowl, a thousand of everything sweet, a thousand of every kind of clothing - for [name], and for all those who came before.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the prepared surface.
  2. Add fresh water.
  3. Read the complete list of names slowly.
  4. After each name, speak: [Name] - this bread is yours. This water is yours. You live.
  5. Sit with memory before eating.
  6. Eat the bread and drink the water as reversion.
- Source note: The thousand-offering formula activated provision through speech. The reversion that followed sustained the living person who gave it. Both acts complete the same provision cycle.

### 7. What They Gave

- Event number: 7
- Section: The Living Memory
- Title: What They Gave
- Words: I have not stolen the cakes of the blessed dead. I carry what they gave. I offer what they carried.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one person among the dead and name one specific thing they gave you.
  2. Ask whether the living have heard that person named with this gift.
  3. Name one way to carry the gift forward more intentionally.
  4. Drink water.
- Source note: Spell 125's prohibition against stealing the dead's cakes was practical: the dead depended on that provision. The living equivalent is the gift received from the dead — carried forward, it sustains; left dormant, it depletes.

- Event number: 8

- Title: What You Will Leave
- Words: A man lives when his name is spoken. Speak the names. They live.

  2. Ask whether that sentence is true of you now, partly true, or not yet true.
- Optional steps:

### 9. The Cycle Confirmed

- Event number: 9
- Section: The Living Memory
- Title: The Cycle Confirmed
- Words: I have made invocation-offerings for the blessed dead. Their names have been spoken. They live. I am their continuation. They are my foundation.
- Steps:
  1. Read the complete list of names one final time.
  2. Speak only the lines that are true.
  3. Say, if true: I have made invocation-offerings for the blessed dead.
  4. Say, if true: Their names have been spoken.
  5. Say, if true: They live.
  6. Say, if true: I am their continuation.
  7. Say, if true: They are my foundation.
  8. Write next year's Wag date somewhere you will see it.
  9. Place water one more time.
  10. Drink it.
- Source note: Harkhuf invited future passers-by to speak the offering formula in exchange for his intercession. The Wag operates on the same exchange: the living tend the dead, and the dead are available as foundation. The cycle closes with water that confirms both sides of the provision.

## 11. The Decan Watch

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_decan_watch_flow.dart
- Key: the-decan-watch
- Tagline: The sky has been counting. Stand under it and add your count.
- Overview: The Decan Watch meets each ten-day boundary through honest sky witness. Outside, inside, clear, or clouded, the user records what the night allowed and names one bearing for the next ten days.
- Confidence: Draws on ten-day decan observation and Kemetic astronomical practice.

### Detail Template

- Title: Detail Template
- Purpose: The opening of [decan name] is a night-sky boundary: the sky has been counting, and one bearing can be taken for the next ten days.
- Words: The Imperishable Stars never set. They mark where I am in time. The decans have counted this night for longer than I can reckon. I stand under them and add my count.
- Steps:
  1. Go outside.
  2. Put the phone down.
  3. Stand under open sky for at least one minute.
  4. Look up.
  5. Face north first for the Imperishable Stars.
  6. Scan the full sky.
  7. Speak the line.
  8. Note the sky in one line.
  9. Open the ḥꜣw day card.
  10. Read the decan name, quality, and Ma'at principle.
  11. Reset intention.
  12. Name one bearing for the coming ten days.
- Outdoor: Go outside if you can. If safety, access, or weather prevents that, stand at a window or threshold. Inside observation still counts; mark the completion as observed from inside. A clouded sky is still a valid record.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

The opening of [decan name] is a night-sky boundary: the sky has been counting, and one bearing can be taken for the next ten days.

Words

6. Scan the full sky.
7. Speak the line.
8. Note the sky in one line.
9. Open the ḥꜣw day card.
10. Read the decan name, quality, and Ma'at principle.
11. Reset intention.
12. Name one bearing for the coming ten days.
```

### Milestone Messages

- One decan month in the Kemetic sky.
- One third of the decan year observed.
- The full decan cycle. The Watchers have revived. The sky has returned.

## 12. The Days Outside the Year

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_days_outside_year_flow.dart
- Key: the-days-outside-the-year
- Tagline: Five days that do not belong to any year. Receive what is born in them.
- Overview: The Days Outside the Year is an annual threshold flow fixed to the Kemetic calendar. It closes the old year, receives the five outside days, and opens Wep Ronpet with one clear intention for what begins.
- Confidence: Draws on the five epagomenal days and their deity births in the Kemetic sacred calendar.

### 0. Set Down What the Year Carried

- Event number: 0
- Kemetic month: 12
- Kemetic day: 30
- Quality: year closing
- Title: Set Down What the Year Carried
- Words: The year has completed its course. What it carried is named. What is finished is finished. What continues, continues.
- Steps:
  1. Place water on the surface as you begin naming.
  2. Name one thing the year gave you that you did not expect.
  3. Name one thing the year asked of you that you did not fully give.
  4. Name one thing that carries across the threshold into the five days.
- Source note: The year-close festival acknowledged that the civil year's work was complete before the five days began. The threshold is crossed clean — what is named is set down at the door, not carried through it.

```text

3. Name one thing the year asked of you that you did not fully give.
4. Name one thing that carries across the threshold into the five days.
```

### 1. Ausar - Restoration

- Event number: 1
- Kemetic month: 13
- Kemetic day: 1
- Netjeru: ausar
- Quality: restoration
- Title: Ausar - Restoration
- Words: Ausar (Osiris) shall live. The akh in Nedit shall live. What was scattered is gathered. What was diminished will endure.
- Steps:
  1. Pause at dawn before ordinary work begins.
  2. Name one thing in the new year that needs to be gathered back.
  3. Say: This needs to be gathered back. Restoration is what I carry.
  4. Place water while you speak.
- Source note: The Osirian pattern is specifically: dispersal, gathering, restoration. Not defeat and victory — the movement of what was whole, what was scattered, and what can be gathered back. This day names what needs gathering before the new year opens.

### 2. Heru Wer - Clear Sight

- Event number: 2
- Kemetic month: 13
- Kemetic day: 2
- Netjeru: heru_wer
- Quality: clear sight
- Title: Heru Wer - Clear Sight
- Words: Heru Wer (Horus the Elder), great god, lord of the sky. The eye that sees the field from above. I receive the quality of clear sight.
- Steps:
  1. Go outside at dawn.
  2. Face east.
  3. Look at the horizon before looking at anything else.
  4. Name what you need to see from a higher position.
  5. Name any rightful position that needs to be claimed by sound action.
  6. Say: Clear sight. Rightful position. I carry these from this day.
- Source note: Heru Wer's power was elevation, not strength. The falcon's height gave it sight that ground-level creatures couldn't have. This day asks what you need to see from that position, and what rightful place in your life requires that clarity.

### 3. Set - Directed Force

- Event number: 3
- Kemetic month: 13
- Kemetic day: 3
- Netjeru: set
- Quality: directed force
- Title: Set - Directed Force
- Words: Set is born. Force and contest enter the world. What contests me sharpens me. I do not run from what is difficult. I place Set's quality in its proper position.
- Steps:
  1. Name one difficult thing you have been avoiding.
  2. Ask where force in you or around you has been misdirected.
  3. Name where that force must be pointed to defend Ma'at.
  4. Say: Directed, it defends. Unplaced, it destroys. I choose the direction.
- Source note: Set's role in the solar journey was essential: he stood at the prow of the solar barge and repelled Apophis. Force correctly placed defends Ma'at. This day gives the force its direction.

### 4. Aset - Effective Speech

- Event number: 4
- Kemetic month: 13
- Kemetic day: 4
- Netjeru: aset
- Quality: effective speech
- Title: Aset - Effective Speech
- Words: Aset (Isis) has come. She speaks to you. Knowledge alone does nothing. Speech alone does nothing. Timed and directed speech produces effect. I carry this into the new year.
- Steps:
  1. Name one true thing you know and have not yet said.
  2. Name what stops the speech: timing, fear, habit, or preparation.
  3. Name the arrangement that must be made before the word is spoken.
  4. Name the preparation your unspoken truth requires.
  5. Say: I carry Aset's quality: to know, wait, and speak with effect.
- Source note: Aset extracted Ra's secret name through precise timing and exact speech — she spoke only what would work, at the moment it would work. The quality she embodies is not just knowledge but effective knowledge: the right thing, said rightly, at the right moment.

## 13. The Open Hand

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_open_hand_flow.dart
- Overview: The Open Hand is a thirty-day outward provision flow with nine sittings. It asks the user to see a real need, give something usable beyond obligation, and confirm what moved without turning generosity into performance.
- Confidence: Draws on the bread-water-clothing-boat formula across Kemetic biography and Spell 125.

### 1. Look Directly

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: See the Need
- Title: Look Directly
- Words: I have given bread to the hungry, water to the thirsty, clothing to the naked, and a boat to the boatless.
- Steps:
  1. Write three names or descriptions of people or situations in your immediate environment where a specific need is present.
  2. For each, name the specific need: food, transport, shelter, attention, connection, skill, or another concrete provision.
  3. Choose one as the first act of this flow.
  4. Write: This ten-day section, I will give ___ to ___.
- Source note: Spell 125 and the virtue autobiographies named specific categories of provision because specificity was the evidence. "I was generous" is not a record. "I gave bread to the hungry" is. This sitting produces the record that the second decan will enact.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"I have given bread to the hungry, water to the thirsty, clothing to the naked, and a boat to the boatless."

Steps
1. Write three names or descriptions of people or situations in your immediate environment where a specific need is present.
2. For each, name the specific need: food, transport, shelter, attention, connection, skill, or another concrete provision.
3. Choose one as the first act of this flow.
4. Write: This ten-day section, I will give ___ to ___.
```

### 2. The First Act

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: See the Need
- Title: The First Act
- Words: The righteous individual is he by whom others are sustained.
- Steps:
  1. Complete the act of provision identified on Day 1 before marking this event observed.
  2. Record what was given and what changed for the other person, even if the change was small.
  3. Name whether any further provision is genuinely yours to continue.
- Source note: Ptahhotep's Maxim 22 defines righteousness by whether others are actually sustained through the person. The record here is the act completed — what changed in someone else's situation because of what you did.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Record what was given and what changed for the other person, even if the change was small.
3. Name whether any further provision is genuinely yours to continue.
```

### 3. Close the First Seeing

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: See the Need
- Title: Close the First Seeing
- Words: He who fosters goodness is a destroyer of evil — as when satisfaction comes and ends hunger, as when clothing ends nakedness, as when water quenches thirst.
- Steps:
  1. Name one local need you had been moving past without seeing.
  2. Name one act of giving in this ten-day section that surprised you.
  3. Name one need you saw but did not act on.
  4. Carry it into the second ten-day section without excuse or editing.
- Source note: The Eloquent Peasant's Third Appeal compares provision to the natural order restoring itself: satisfaction ending hunger, clothing ending nakedness — specific and physical. The closing names where the restoration was real and where it was only witnessed.

  2. For each act, write what will be given and who or what receives it.
  3. Write when each act will happen before Day 19.

## 14. The Djed

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_djed_flow.dart
- Key: the-djed
- Tagline: What holds you upright when everything around you shakes?
- Overview: The Djed is a thirty-day stability flow with nine sittings. It names what holds life upright, meets what threatens that structure, and closes with one concrete act of raising, repair, or restored support.
- Confidence: Draws on the Djed pillar, annual raising, stability, contest, and uprightness.

### 1. What Is Your Spine?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: name_the_spine
- Title: What Is Your Spine?
- Words: Become established, become established, established one. Your identity will endure with people even as your identity comes to be with the gods.
- Steps:
  1. Name four or five load-bearing elements of your life right now: practices, relationships, commitments, structures, or truths.
  2. For each, mark the current condition: solid, under pressure, or wobbling.
  3. Circle the wobbling elements. These become the material for the mock battle.
- Source note: The Djed pillar was Ausar's backbone — what remained when everything else was scattered. Your spine is the same: what persists when the rest is under pressure. Name it before the mock battle begins.


  2. Name when the wobble began: what changed, stopped, or accumulated?
  3. Name one real thing that would return this element toward solid.

## 15. The Reading House

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/the_reading_house_flow.dart
- Key: the-reading-house
- Tagline: A private-study foundation for one book.
- Overview:
  1. The Reading House
  2. A house gathers around one book. Whoever opens the house is the host — they name the book, set the reading, and shape the sittings the house moves through together.
  3. Each sitting opens the same way: a section to read, a theme to hold, and a question to sit with. You read on your own first. What you write in your margin is yours — private by default, never shared unless you choose to share it.
  4. Before you mark a sitting complete, you say where you are: Carrying the reading forward, or Not yet. Carrying is the house's quiet signal that you're moving through the book together, at your own pace, in your own time.
  5. When you choose to, you can offer one fragment of your reading to the house — a line that struck you, a thought worth passing on. Others can respond, once, in the shared margin. The house keeps what is offered. Nothing is counted, ranked, or performed.
  6. A reading house can be kept solo — guided study, just you and the book — or opened to company, a small group moving through the same text in the tradition of the Per-Ankh, where reading was never done alone.

### 1. Open the Text

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Opening section
- Title: Open the Text
- Theme: What is the text asking you to carry?
- Private prompt: Before company shapes the reading, what is this opening asking you to hold privately?
- Host note: Begin with your own encounter. The house can receive a fragment later.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Private Prompt
Before company shapes the reading, what is this opening asking you to hold privately?

Host Note
Begin with your own encounter. The house can receive a fragment later.
```

- Section: Middle section
- Title: Hold the Middle

```text
Private Prompt
```

- Title: Seal the Reading

- Private prompt: Mark what remains after the book closes: sentence, question, practice, or passage.
- Host note: Choose one fragment privately. Shared surfaces come later.

## 16. Legacy Evening Threshold

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/evening_threshold_flow.dart
- Key: evening_threshold
- Tagline: You named one measure this morning. Before the day closes, place it on the scale.
- Overview: The Evening Threshold is a carry-and-crossing practice. You name one thing to carry into the day, land it honestly at evening, then decide the next morning whether it crosses forward or is released. What is carried crosses intentionally. What is released is left at the threshold completely.
- Enrollment copy: Name what you carry today. Tonight you land it honestly; tomorrow morning you choose whether it crosses forward or is released.

### 1. How did it land?

- Event number: 1
- Title: How did it land?
- Purpose: You held something this morning. Before the day closes, place it on the scale - not to judge it, but to know what was true.
- Words: This was what I named. This is what I met.
- Delivery: Read your morning return aloud or silently. Pause. Do not rush this. The day is still happening. Let it settle before you answer.
- Source note: The Kemetic evening rite was not confession. It was reckoning: placing what occurred beside what was intended, and seeing the difference clearly.
- Completion labels:
  - I held it.
  - I slipped.
  - I'm still working on it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
You held something this morning. Before the day closes, place it on the scale - not to judge it, but to know what was true.

Words
"This was what I named. This is what I met."

Delivery
Read your morning return aloud or silently. Pause. Do not rush this. The day is still happening. Let it settle before you answer.
```

### 2. What crosses with you?

- Event number: 2
- Title: What crosses with you?
- Purpose: Morning stands on the other side of the night. Choose whether yesterday's carry crosses forward or is released.
- Words: What I carry, I carry with intention. What I release, I release completely.
- Delivery: Read yesterday clearly. Then choose what enters today before the day gathers speed.
- Source note: The threshold in Kemetic architecture was a ritual boundary. What crossed was chosen, named, and intentional. Nothing drifted through.
- Completion labels:
  - Carry it forward.
  - Release it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose

Delivery

```

## 17. The Fair Hearing

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-fair-hearing
- Tagline: You are a balance. If it wavers, then you will waver.
- Overview: A 30-day practice of fair judgment: hear fully, keep one measure for favored and unfavored sides, and pronounce the decision clearly.
- Confidence: Draws on Kemetic wisdom and administrative scenes of hearing, weighing, and pronounced judgment.
- Routing summary: Best for judgment, partiality, premature decisions, deferred decisions, and uneven hearing.

### 1. Who Is Before You?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Where Are You Called to Judge?
- Title: Who Is Before You?
- Purpose: The hearing begins before you think it does. The view formed before the full account is heard is already shaping what you will hear. This sitting names that view. The strongest preference marks where the fair hearing is most needed.
- Words: Be patient, so that you may learn Ma'at. Control your own preference, so that the humble petitioner may gain.
- Steps:
  1. List the work, family, friendship, community, or self-judgment situations where your view affects an outcome.
  2. For each, mark whether you have already formed a view before fully hearing all sides.
  3. Circle the situation where your preference is strongest.
- Source note: Khunanup appealed nine times because the first hearing produced no decision. The petitioner must persist; the judge must eventually decide. This sitting names who is waiting for a real hearing.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

The hearing begins before you think it does. The view formed before the full account is heard is already shaping what you will hear. This sitting names that view. The strongest preference marks where the fair hearing is most needed.

Words
"Be patient, so that you may learn Ma'at. Control your own preference, so that the humble petitioner may gain."

Steps
1. List the work, family, friendship, community, or self-judgment situations where your view affects an outcome.
2. For each, mark whether you have already formed a view before fully hearing all sides.
3. Circle the situation where your preference is strongest.
```

### 2. Your Known Preferences

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Where Are You Called to Judge?
- Title: Your Known Preferences
- Purpose: A named preference can be held lightly. An unnamed preference tilts the balance while pretending not to exist.
- Words: Control your own preference, so that the humble petitioner may gain.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the most charged situation from Day 1 and name what you prefer to be true.
  2. Name who has more power and who has less.
  3. Name whether your preference aligns with the more powerful party.
  4. Write: My preference in [situation] is [specific view]. I will hold this preference lightly until I have heard fully.

```text
2. Name who has more power and who has less.
3. Name whether your preference aligns with the more powerful party.
4. Write: My preference in [situation] is [specific view]. I will hold this preference lightly until I have heard fully.
```

### 3. The Inventory Complete

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Where Are You Called to Judge?
- Title: The Inventory Complete
- Purpose: One live judgment receives the second ten-day section of fair-hearing practice.
- Words: Do not be partial, and do not give in to a whim. Let your decision be pronounced.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one situation for the second ten-day section - the one where fairness will require the most from you.
  2. Name who has not yet been fully heard.
  3. Write: In the second ten-day section, I will give the full fair hearing to [situation]. I will hear [person or side] before I decide.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
2. Name who has not yet been fully heard.
3. Write: In the second ten-day section, I will give the full fair hearing to [situation]. I will hear [person or side] before I decide.
```

### 4. Hear Fully

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Practice of Fair Hearing
- Title: Hear Fully
- Purpose: Ptahhotep said the wronged person needs to unburden the heart even when not every request can be granted. The full hearing is itself a form of justice, before the decision. A conclusion formed while the person is still speaking is premature.
- Words: Be patient when listening to the words of a petitioner. Do not dismiss him until he has completely unburdened himself.
- Steps:
  1. Before the hearing, write one sentence about what you expect to hear.
  2. Give the actual hearing.
  3. Do not interrupt.
  4. Do not signal or state your conclusion while the person is still speaking.
  5. Afterward, write one thing you heard that complicated or expanded your view.
- Source note: Ptahhotep's instruction is specific: do not dismiss the petitioner before they have completely unburdened themselves. A hearing that stops when the judge has enough for their existing conclusion is not a fair hearing — it is an efficient confirmation.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
Ptahhotep said the wronged person needs to unburden the heart even when not every request can be granted. The full hearing is itself a form of justice, before the decision. A conclusion formed while the person is still speaking is premature.

Words
"Be patient when listening to the words of a petitioner. Do not dismiss him until he has completely unburdened himself."

Steps
1. Before the hearing, write one sentence about what you expect to hear.
2. Give the actual hearing.
3. Do not interrupt.
4. Do not signal or state your conclusion while the person is still speaking.
5. Afterward, write one thing you heard that complicated or expanded your view.
```

### 5. Apply the Same Measure

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: The Practice of Fair Hearing
- Title: Apply the Same Measure
- Purpose: The same measure must hold when a different person is standing on it.
- Words: Do not cover your face against one whom you know; do not blind your sight against one whom you have seen.
- Steps:
  1. Test whether you would hear the claim the same way if the parties were reversed.
  2. Name one piece of evidence you have minimized because of who offered it.
  3. Write: I have been minimizing [account] because [reason]. I will hear it again with the same attention.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
2. Name one piece of evidence you have minimized because of who offered it.
3. Write: I have been minimizing [account] because [reason]. I will hear it again with the same attention.
```

### 6. Pronounce the Decision

- Event number: 6
- Flow day: 19
- Section: The Practice of Fair Hearing
- Title: Pronounce the Decision
- Purpose: Indefinite postponement wearing the mask of patience is not patience — it is a different form of injustice. The hearing is complete when the decision is named clearly to those it affects.
- Words: Turn away from this slothfulness, and let your decision be pronounced.
- Steps:
  1. State the decision you can give now based on what has been heard.
  2. Write: Based on what I have heard, [decision]. This is based on [basis].
  3. If it has not been communicated, name exactly when it will be spoken.
- Source note: The target is not the dramatic silence of a judge prolonging a case for benefit. It is the ordinary silence of a decision deferred because pronouncing it is uncomfortable.
- Completion labels:
  - Decision pronounced

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Words

2. Write: Based on what I have heard, [decision]. This is based on [basis].
3. If it has not been communicated, name exactly when it will be spoken.
```

### 7. Was It Fair?

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: The Decision Pronounced
- Title: Was It Fair?
- Purpose: The process is reviewed by whether the hearing was fair, not by whether the outcome favored everyone.
- Words: Do not utter falsehood, for you are a balance. If it wavers, then you will waver.
- Steps:
  1. Ask whether the hearing was full.
  2. Ask whether the measure was consistent.
  3. Ask whether the decision was based on what was heard.
  4. For any no or partial answer, name exactly what was missing.
  5. If something was not fully heard, name whether an acknowledgment is owed.

```text
3. Ask whether the decision was based on what was heard.
4. For any no or partial answer, name exactly what was missing.
5. If something was not fully heard, name whether an acknowledgment is owed.
```

### 8. The Petitioner Not Heard

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: The Decision Pronounced
- Title: The Petitioner Not Heard
- Purpose: The practice looks beyond the main case to the person whose appeal has simply not been heard.
- Words: Do not be a hawk to the commoners. One who is deaf to Ma'at has no friend.
- Steps:
  1. Name someone who has appealed to you and genuinely has not been heard.
  2. Name what they are asking and why they have not been heard.
  3. Name what a fair hearing would require before the closing sitting.

### 9. The Balance Holds

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Decision Pronounced
- Title: The Balance Holds
- Purpose: The balance that wavered during this cycle is not a failure — it is information. The closing names where the balance held and where it didn't, and what practice will correct the latter.
- Words: Do not utter falsehood, for you are a balance. Great is Ma'at, lasting in effect.
- Steps:
  1. Name the situation where the fair hearing was most complete.
  2. Name the situation where partiality or incompleteness still entered.
  3. Speak only the lines that are true.
  4. Say, if true: I heard fully.
  5. Say, if true: I applied the same measure.
  6. Say, if true: I pronounced a decision clearly.
  7. Write one continuing practice: When I am called to judge, I will [practice] before I decide.
- Source note: The Eloquent Peasant's nine appeals end with justice finally pronounced and goods restored. The balance protects both the judged and the judge. The judge who hears unfairly does not only harm the petitioner — they become an unreliable measure.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The balance that wavered during this cycle is not a failure — it is information. The closing names where the balance held and where it didn't, and what practice will correct the latter.

Words
"Do not utter falsehood, for you are a balance. Great is Ma'at, lasting in effect."

Steps
1. Name the situation where the fair hearing was most complete.
2. Name the situation where partiality or incompleteness still entered.
3. Speak only the lines that are true.
4. Say, if true: I heard fully.
5. Say, if true: I applied the same measure.
6. Say, if true: I pronounced a decision clearly.
7. Write one continuing practice: When I am called to judge, I will [practice] before I decide.
```

## 18. The First Arrangement

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-first-arrangement
- Tagline: For I ordered everything in its proper place.
- Overview: A 30-day space-order practice: choose one place, see what belongs, remove what does not, and establish maintenance.
- Confidence: The First Arrangement draws on Zep Tepi, temple order, offering-table placement, and purification practice.
- Routing summary: Best for clutter, physical space, room, desk, office, studio, home, mess, organization, focus, environment, and physical reset.

### 1. See What Is There

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: See the Space
- Title: See What Is There
- Purpose: The space cannot be arranged until it is seen accurately — as it is right now, with every accumulated thing in its current position.
- Words: Order begins with seeing what is actually there.
- Steps:
  1. Stand at the entrance of your chosen space.
  2. Write what is actually there, where it sits, and what the current arrangement communicates.
  3. Do not clean yet.
- Source note: Temple restoration texts began by inventorying what was present before anything was moved. The unseen object cannot be placed correctly.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

The space cannot be arranged until it is seen accurately — as it is right now, with every accumulated thing in its current position.

Words
"Order begins with seeing what is actually there."

Steps
1. Stand at the entrance of your chosen space.
2. Write what is actually there, where it sits, and what the current arrangement communicates.
3. Do not clean yet.
```

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: See the Space
- Title: What Is This Space For?
- Purpose: Define the space by purpose rather than preference.
- Words: Every place has a right relation.
- Steps:
  1. Define the space’s true purpose in one or two precise sentences.
  2. Write what this space should make easier.
  3. Name the quality of attention it should support.

#### Canonical Detail Text


- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: See the Space
- Title: What Is Isfet in This Space?
- Purpose: Mark what belongs, what does not, and what is misplaced.
- Words: Disorder can be named before it is removed.
- Steps:
  1. Review the inventory.
  2. Mark each item: belongs here, does not belong here, or belongs but is misplaced.
  3. Do not remove anything yet.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Mark what belongs, what does not, and what is misplaced.

2. Mark each item: belongs here, does not belong here, or belongs but is misplaced.
3. Do not remove anything yet.
```

### 4. Remove What Doesn’t Belong

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Clear the Space
- Title: Remove What Doesn’t Belong
- Purpose: Removal is the active half of ordering. The object in the wrong space does the same damage to the room's function as the boundary stone in the wrong field. A hallway pile or same-room corner keeps the false relation in place.
- Words: What does not belong here is set in its true place.
- Steps:
  1. Physically remove every item marked "does not belong here."
  2. Put each item where it truly belongs, not in the hallway or a corner of the same room.
  3. If it belongs nowhere you inhabit, discard it or release it.
  4. Record what remains.
- Completion labels:
  - Cleared

  2. Name the entry point.
  3. Decide how everything else will relate to these two points.

### 6. The Arrangement

- Event number: 6
- Flow day: 19
- Section: Clear the Space
- Title: The Arrangement
- Purpose: Zep Tepi was not the creation of new things but the placing of existing things in right relationship. Each item goes where it belongs in relation to the center and the threshold.
- Words: For I ordered everything in its proper place.
- Steps:
  1. Arrange what remains by purpose, proximity, clear path, clean surfaces, and orientation.
  2. Stand at the threshold.
  3. Write what the space now says.
- Source note: "For I ordered everything in its proper place" — the Kemetic standard for good governance applied to any space. The arrangement is relational first: what is in right relationship to the center, what is in right relationship to the entry, what has a clear path between them.
- Completion labels:
  - Arranged

- Words: Water, air, and scent return the space to clean order.
- Steps:

  2. Wipe surfaces with water.
  3. Add one intentional scent.
  4. Record the state of the space after purification.

- Event number: 8

- Title: Inhabit the Space
- Purpose: Use the space and listen to what the arrangement enables.
- Words: The arranged space is tested by use.
- Steps:
  1. Use the space for its main purpose.
  2. Do not adjust while using it.
  3. Afterward, write what was easier.
  4. Write what remains misaligned and what the space communicated.

### 9. The Maintenance Practice

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Maintain the Space
- Title: The Maintenance Practice
- Purpose: The ordering established in the second ten-day section returns to disorder without maintenance. The five-minute daily practice is what makes the ordering permanent rather than a one-time reset. The instruction must be specific enough to follow without thinking about it.
- Words: What is arranged must be returned to arrangement.
- Steps:
  1. Write the maintenance practice as a specific instruction: When I [enter/leave] this space each [morning/evening], I will [specific acts].
  2. Include how the practice returns objects.
  3. Include how the practice clears surfaces and performs one sensory act of purification.
  4. Share only the one-sentence statement of what the space now communicates.
- Source note: The offering ritual was daily — not because the offering expired, but because the space required continued attention to remain ordered. What is arranged once and not maintained is not an ordered space. It is a space that was arranged once.
- Completion labels:
  - Maintenance established

## 19. The Living Pattern

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-living-pattern
- Tagline: The natural world is a text. Patient observation reveals one lesson at a time.
- Overview: A 30-day observation practice: watch one natural subject until a real pattern appears, then act from its principle.
- Confidence: The Living Pattern reads natural phenomena as demonstrations of durable principles, beginning with observation before interpretation.
- Routing summary: Best for nature, observation, animals, water, vegetation, night sky, moon, stars, patience, pattern, and natural lessons.

### 1. Choose the Subject

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Observation
- Title: Choose the Subject
- Purpose: The Kemetic observer who watched the jackal did not decide in advance what it would mean. They watched. This rite begins the same way: choose the subject and look at it without deciding what it demonstrates.
- Words: Say what is. Do not interpret yet.
- Steps:
  1. Go to your subject and observe for 15-20 minutes.
  2. Write only what actually happens.
  3. Do not interpret yet.
- Source note: The Anubis theology emerged from watching actual jackals at the boundary between living fields and the desert necropolis. The behavior came first; the principle followed. Choose the subject and begin watching — interpretation is Decan 2's work.

```text

Words
"Say what is. Do not interpret yet."

Steps
1. Go to your subject and observe for 15-20 minutes.
2. Write only what actually happens.
3. Do not interpret yet.
```

  2. Record what repeated from Day 1 and what changed.
  3. Separate constants from variables. Do not interpret yet.

## 20. The House of Life

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-house-of-life
- Tagline: What you learn accurately, you give to those who come after.
- Overview: A 30-day scribal practice: learn accurately, write and recite one useful piece of knowledge, then transmit it with care.
- Confidence: Draws on Per Ankh learning, scribal copying, temple practice, and careful transmission.
- Routing summary: Best for learning, accurate transmission, knowledge discipline, and users ready for a positive practice flow.

### 1. Enter the House of Life

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Enter the Per Ankh
- Title: Enter the House of Life
- Purpose: The Per Ankh was a working scriptorium — not a library where the learned went to read, but a workshop where texts were produced, tested, and transmitted. This sitting enters it as a practitioner, not a browser. The weakest discipline is where the practice begins.
- Words: Come to me, Djehuty, that you may give advice and make me skillful in your office.
- Steps:
  1. Name what you are currently learning - a skill, craft, practice, tradition, or body of knowledge.
  2. Check the three disciplines: writing it down, speaking or reciting it, and seeking those who know more.
  3. Name the weakest of the three.
- Source note: The Per Ankh's priests were not passive custodians — they copied, produced, interpreted, and applied what they held. Knowledge that sits in the archive without being used is not living knowledge. This sitting names what you are actively working with, and where the work is weakest.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Words
"Come to me, Djehuty, that you may give advice and make me skillful in your office."

Steps
1. Name what you are currently learning - a skill, craft, practice, tradition, or body of knowledge.
2. Check the three disciplines: writing it down, speaking or reciting it, and seeking those who know more.
3. Name the weakest of the three.
```

### 2. Neither Take Away Nor Add

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Enter the Per Ankh
- Title: Neither Take Away Nor Add
- Purpose: Accuracy begins by seeing whether the subject is being received as it is or bent toward what you already believe.
- Words: Say what he said. Neither take away nor add to it.
- Steps:
  1. Name where you are adding: interpreting early, filling gaps, or importing your own agenda.
  2. Name where you are taking away: simplifying too far or avoiding what challenges you.
  3. Write one sentence about where accuracy is most compromised.

#### Canonical Detail Text


- Steps:
  1. Write: In the second ten-day section, I will practice writing, reciting, and seeking on [subject].
  2. Name one person, text, teacher, or practitioner who knows more than you.
  3. Name one thing you will write down this week with enough clarity for another reader.

### 4. Write With Your Hand

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Three Disciplines
- Title: Write With Your Hand
- Purpose: What you can write precisely has been learned. What you can only gesture at is still loose in the understanding. Writing is the test. A specific gap is the Per Ankh's next assignment, not a failure.
- Words: Write with your hand, recite with your mouth, and converse with those more knowledgeable than you.
- Steps:
  1. Write one complete account of something you have learned, as if the reader cannot ask you to clarify.
  2. Read it back and correct anything that depends on you standing beside the text.
  3. Name the specific gap the writing revealed in your understanding.
- Source note: The Kemetic scribe was valued for the accuracy of the hand — the transmission that neither added nor removed. Writing what you know precisely enough for a future reader who cannot ask you to clarify is the scribal standard. Where the writing breaks down is where the learning is still incomplete.

```text
2. Read it back and correct anything that depends on you standing beside the text.
3. Name the specific gap the writing revealed in your understanding.
```

### 5. Recite With Your Mouth

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: The Three Disciplines
- Title: Recite With Your Mouth
- Purpose: The scribe who could recite what they copied had internalized it. The scribe who could only locate it in the text had stored it, not learned it. Collapse is useful data because it shows where understanding still depends on the text rather than on itself.
- Words: Write with your hand, recite with your mouth, and converse with those more knowledgeable than you.
- Steps:
  1. Speak one concept or principle from memory, accurately and in order.
  2. Mark what stays fluent when spoken.
  3. Mark what collapses when spoken.
  4. If something collapses, write the exact sentence, term, or sequence that needs repair.
  5. If someone asks a question you cannot answer accurately, write it as the next thing to learn.

#### Canonical Detail Text

- Words: I am the servant of your house. Come to me that you may advise me.
- Steps:
  1. Seek the person, book, source, teacher, or practitioner named on Day 9.
  2. Ask one question your current study cannot answer.
  3. Write one thing you learned that you could not have gotten from yourself alone.
- Source note: The instruction texts frame the student's relationship to the teacher as: come, advise me, let me be your servant. The posture of seeking is part of the practice. The question you bring determines what you receive.

  2. Name one person who could benefit from receiving it.
  3. Choose the form: conversation, written note, demonstration, source recommendation, or another clear transmission.

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: The Transmission
- Title: The Act of Transmission
- Purpose: The transmission promised on Day 21 becomes living knowledge only when it is given, received, and recorded honestly.
- Words: Neither take away nor add to it. Give what was learned to those who will receive it.
- Steps:
  1. If the transmission happened, write what you gave and to whom.
  2. Ask what the recipient understood and what confused them.
  3. If the transmission has not happened, do it today.
  4. If it cannot happen today, name exactly when before the closing sitting.

### 9. The House of Life Holds

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Transmission
- Title: The House of Life Holds
- Purpose: The House of Life closes with one precise sentence on the subject studied and one practice that continues. The practice that continues is more important than the thirty-day record.
- Words: Writing was their memory-priest. Better is this profession than all professions. It makes people great.
- Steps:
  1. Name what you can now say with precision on the subject you studied.
  2. Name what remains beyond this 30-day cycle and how you will keep learning it.
  3. Speak only the lines that are true.
  4. Say, if true: I wrote.
  5. Say, if true: I recited.
  6. Say, if true: I sought.
  7. Say, if true: I transmitted.
  8. Say, if true: What I know is more accurate.
  9. Open one ḥꜣw node entry and read slowly, as the scribe reads.
- Source note: Chester Beatty IV called the scribes' writing their memory-priest — the mechanism of their continued existence. The precise sentence that closes this flow is the user's contribution to the same chain. What you can now say accurately, you can now transmit.
- Completion labels:
  - Transmitted

## 21. The Boundary Stone

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-boundary-stone
- Tagline: What is yours ends somewhere. Know where.
- Overview: A 30-day boundary practice: map resources, labor, credit, and force; name what moved; restore one marker to its place.
- Confidence: Draws on boundary-stone language in administrative and wisdom settings.
- Routing summary: Best for restraint, excess, taking more than is due, moved boundaries, credit, labor, resources, and disproportionate force.

### 1. Name the Fields

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Map the Stones
- Title: Name the Fields
- Purpose: After the Nile flood, the boundaries were resurveyed because the water had moved them. The stone was replaced not according to memory but according to the original measurement. The four fields are mapped from the same starting question: what is actually mine?
- Words: Do not move the markers on the boundaries of the fields, nor alter the measuring line.
- Steps:
  1. Map four domains: resources, labor, credit, and force.
  2. For each, write what is actually yours and what belongs to others.
  3. For force, name what level of pressure or authority is proportionate to the situation.
- Source note: The boundary-stone surveyors worked from original records, not from what was convenient after the flood. This sitting maps four fields — resources, labor, credit, force — from the same starting question: what is actually mine, and where does someone else begin?

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose

Words
"Do not move the markers on the boundaries of the fields, nor alter the measuring line."

Steps
1. Map four domains: resources, labor, credit, and force.
2. For each, write what is actually yours and what belongs to others.
3. For force, name what level of pressure or authority is proportionate to the situation.
```

### 2. The Measure of Each Field

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Map the Stones
- Title: The Measure of Each Field
- Purpose: Enough is the measure in each domain - not the maximum that can be taken.
- Words: Do not be covetous for a single cubit. The measure is exact.
- Steps:
  1. Return to resources, labor, credit, and force.
  2. For each domain, write what enough looks like.
  3. Mark any domain where the measure is genuinely unclear.
  4. Name where you may be taking from not knowing the line rather than from actual entitlement.

```text
4. Name where you may be taking from not knowing the line rather than from actual entitlement.
```

### 3. First Survey Complete

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Map the Stones
- Title: First Survey Complete
- Purpose: The first map points to one domain for honest survey in the second ten-day section.
- Words: The boundary of the fields is set. What is mine is named. What is not mine is also named.
- Steps:
  1. Look at the map.
  2. Name which field is larger, smaller, or more contested than you thought.
  3. Name one domain where you suspect the stone has moved.
  4. Write: In the second ten-day section, I will look honestly at [domain].


### 4. The Honest Survey

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Assess the Survey
- Title: The Honest Survey
- Purpose: The resurvey asked one question per field: where does the stone actually belong? Not where it ended up after the flood — where the original measurement said it should be.
- Words: One who transgresses the furrow shortens a lifetime.
- Steps:
  1. For resources, ask whether you are consuming at the level that is actually yours.
  2. For labor, ask whether you are doing your work, someone else's work, or leaving yours to others.
  3. For credit, ask whether you are claiming what was shared or someone else's.
  4. For force, ask whether pressure or authority has continued past what the situation required.
- Source note: The re-survey after the Nile flood was restorative, not creative — it found where the stone belonged before disruption moved it. Not where the boundary has drifted, but where it should be if the original measure were applied honestly.

```text
3. For credit, ask whether you are claiming what was shared or someone else's.
4. For force, ask whether pressure or authority has continued past what the situation required.
```

### 5. The Gullet's Evidence

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Assess the Survey
- Title: The Gullet's Evidence
- Purpose: What was taken past the measure is already being returned through a different mechanism. The gullet's rejection is not a metaphor — it is the actual consequence operating in the relationship, the situation, or the body of the one who took too much.
- Words: The property of a dependent is an obstruction to the throat. Too much bread is swallowed and spat up.
- Steps:
  1. Where the stone moved, name what life is already throwing back: strain, resentment, depletion, or loss of trust.
  2. Name the evidence without defending it.
  3. Write what enough would have looked like in that domain.
- Source note: Amenemope's image of the gullet rejecting what was taken past measure is viscerally physical — bread swallowed and spat up, property that becomes an obstruction in the throat. The excess does not stay. It produces visible evidence that the measure was exceeded.

- Title: Survey Findings

- Steps:
  1. For each moved stone, name what was taken beyond the measure and by how much.
  2. For each, name what restoration would look like in exact words.
  3. Carry these restorations into the third ten-day section as commitments.

### 7. Begin the Restoration

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Restore the Survey
- Title: Begin the Restoration
- Purpose: Restoration begins with the moved stone that most strained relationship or trust.
- Words: Guard yourself against the blemish of greediness. He who forsakes his relatives is truly poor.
- Steps:
  1. Choose the moved stone that caused the most relational damage.
  2. Write: To restore [domain], I will [specific act] by [date].
  3. Name the actual proportionate measure.
  4. Write where the stone belongs.
- Source note: Ptahhotep treats greed as a relationship disease. The stone placed correctly is not loss; it is the beginning of restored trust.

- Title: The First Stone Placed
- Purpose: The first restoration check names whether the Day 21 act happened and locates one simpler restoration before the closing sitting.
- Words: Do not be selfish in the division. Greater is the claim of the good-natured person than the assertive.
- Steps:
  1. If the restoration happened, write what changed.
  2. If not, name exactly what is between you and the restoration.
  3. Choose a second, simpler stone that can be restored before the closing sitting.

### 9. The Survey Complete

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Restore the Survey
- Title: The Survey Complete
- Purpose: The survey closes with the stones named: placed, restored, or still displaced. What remains displaced is a named commitment — which is different from an unexamined situation.
- Words: What is mine I hold. What is not mine I have set down. The fields are measured. The stones are placed.
- Steps:
  1. For each domain, write: stone in right place, stone restored this cycle, or stone still displaced.
  2. For each restored stone, name what shifted in the relationship or situation.
  3. For any stone still displaced, name what prevents restoration and the next measure.
- Source note: The Kemetic resurvey was complete when every stone was accounted for — placed, replaced, or noted as requiring further work. What remains displaced has been located and named, which is the prerequisite for restoration.
- Completion labels:
  - Stones placed

## 22. Hotep

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: hotep
- Tagline: What you have given is enough. Cool your heart.
- Overview: A 30-day evening peace practice: name what was given, separate real obligation from fear, and let the heart cool before sleep.
- Confidence: Draws on htp offering language, cooling passages, and the peace of completed presentation.
- Routing summary: Best for inability to stop, compulsive overwork, fear of tomorrow, and the feeling that the offering is never enough.

### 1. Name the Offering

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Name the Offering
- Title: Name the Offering
- Purpose: The offering is complete when it meets the measure of what was owed — not when the giver has given everything possible. Hotep does not happen from a working position; the body must be allowed to sit where the offering can close. This distinction is the practice.
- Words: I have come having gotten Horus's eye, that your heart may become cool with it.
- Steps:
  1. Place water on your surface.
  2. Sit somewhere you actually rest, not in a working position.
  3. Write what you are currently offering: time, labor, care, attention, skill.
  4. Write what is owed.
  5. Drink the water.
- Source note: The Pyramid Texts begin restoration with cool water because the heart that has been cooled can receive the offering's completion. The cool heart is what Hotep produces — not what you must bring to it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The offering is complete when it meets the measure of what was owed — not when the giver has given everything possible. Hotep does not happen from a working position; the body must be allowed to sit where the offering can close. This distinction is the practice.

Words
"I have come having gotten Horus's eye, that your heart may become cool with it."

Steps
1. Place water on your surface.
2. Sit somewhere you actually rest, not in a working position.
3. Write what you are currently offering: time, labor, care, attention, skill.
4. Write what is owed.
5. Drink the water.
```

### 2. The Measure of the Offering

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Name the Offering
- Title: The Measure of the Offering
- Purpose: Distinguish an actual gap in what is owed from the belief that more is always owed.
- Words: Accept the outflow that comes from you. Your heart will not become weary with it.
- Steps:
  1. Return to what you wrote on Day 1.
  2. Ask whether there is a real gap between what was owed and what has been given.
  3. Write one sentence about the gap, or its absence.

```text
3. Write one sentence about the gap, or its absence.
```

### 3. The Offering Inventory Complete

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Name the Offering
- Title: The Offering Inventory Complete
- Purpose: Name what is keeping the heart hot before the completion question begins.
- Words: The eye has been gotten. The water is cool. What was offered has been offered.
- Steps:
  1. Name what is producing the inability to rest or fear of tomorrow.
  2. Ask whether it is real incompleteness or the distortion that more is always owed.
  3. Carry that distinction into the next ten-day section.


### 4. The Question: Is It Done?

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Is It Complete?
- Title: The Question: Is It Done?
- Purpose: The central question of Hotep is not "have I done enough" — it is "have I done what was owed." These produce different answers.
- Words: Do not go to bed fearing tomorrow. God is success; man is failure.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the line before the question.
  2. Sit with the question for at least two minutes: Has the offering of this period been made?
  3. Answer with the Day 1 measure, not with a vague feeling of enough.
  4. Write: The offering is complete, or The offering is incomplete in [specific way].
- Source note: Amenemope does not address fear of tomorrow with productivity advice. The answer is distinguishing between what is yours to do and what is tomorrow's problem. The offering made today closes today's account.

```text
Words

Steps

2. Sit with the question for at least two minutes: Has the offering of this period been made?
3. Answer with the Day 1 measure, not with a vague feeling of enough.
4. Write: The offering is complete, or The offering is incomplete in [specific way].
```

- Event number: 5

- Section: Is It Complete?
- Title: What You Cannot Control
- Purpose: Name what you are carrying to bed that your anxiety cannot actually change. What remains after that sorting is the real offering.
- Words: Man knows not what tomorrow will be. What I cannot control, I set down here.
- Steps:
  1. Write the outcomes, choices, or circumstances you are carrying to bed but cannot control.
  2. Draw a line through each one.
  3. Place the page away from the bed.
  4. Name what remains that is genuinely yours.

  3. If complete, write: What was owed has been given. The heart is owed its cooling.

  2. Name one thing you set down that would usually have stayed in the bed with you.

### 9. The Cool Heart

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Receive the Hotep
- Title: The Cool Heart
- Purpose: The cool heart is the product of the completed offering, not its prerequisite. This sitting produces the cool heart by closing the offering record and placing what cannot be controlled outside the sleeping space. The act is physical because the cool heart is physical.
- Words: These your cool waters have come from your son. Your heart will not become weary with it. Hotep.
- Steps:
  1. Place water where you will be before sleep.
  2. Sit where you will be before sleep.
  3. Name aloud what you offered across this flow: time, labor, care, presence, skill.
  4. Speak: What was owed has been given.
  5. Write anything left in the bed that is not yours to control.
  6. Place the paper away from where you sleep.
  7. Drink the water.
  8. Lie down to sleep.
- Source note: The ḥtp formula began with "Hotep" — satisfaction received, offering made — and the provision followed. The peace comes first; the provision follows from it. This final event replicates that sequence: the record is closed, the water is drunk, the heart is cooled.
- Completion labels:
  - Cooled

## 23. The Open Mouth

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-open-mouth
- Tagline: What comes out of your mouth is what you are creating.
- Overview: A 30-day speech practice: record what the mouth creates, govern one word, and speak or withhold with discipline.
- Confidence: Draws on the formative power of speech in the Memphite Theology and Opening of the Mouth ritual.
- Routing summary: Best for heated speech, hasty response, slander, careless words, and important truth left unsaid.

### 1. Open the Mouth's Record

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Inventory
- Title: Open the Mouth's Record
- Purpose: What comes out of the mouth before the heart has governed it is not yet speech — it is reflex with sound. This sitting finds the reflex patterns before they are taken for speech.
- Words: It is the heart and the tongue that have power. I open the record of what my tongue has been commanding.
- Steps:
  1. Write what your mouth produces when you are tired, frustrated, or thoughtless.
  2. Name what you are saying that should not be said.
  3. Name what is inaccurate or overheated.
  4. Name what needs to be said but has been swallowed.
- Source note: In the Memphite Theology, the heart conceives and the tongue commands — formation happens through speech. The mouth that produces without the heart's governance is not creating order; it is creating noise. This sitting opens the record of which category most of the recent speech has fallen into.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
What comes out of the mouth before the heart has governed it is not yet speech — it is reflex with sound. This sitting finds the reflex patterns before they are taken for speech.

Words
"It is the heart and the tongue that have power. I open the record of what my tongue has been commanding."

Steps
1. Write what your mouth produces when you are tired, frustrated, or thoughtless.
2. Name what you are saying that should not be said.
3. Name what is inaccurate or overheated.
4. Name what needs to be said but has been swallowed.
```

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: The Inventory
- Title: What Has Your Mouth Created?
- Purpose: Look at one thing said and one thing not said, then name what each created.
- Words: My speech has not been heated. My heart has not been hasty.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one piece of speech from the last five days.
  2. Write what it created.
  3. Choose one thing not said.
  4. Write what its absence created.
  5. Name the speech pattern from Day 1 that the next ten-day section should govern.

```text
2. Write what it created.
3. Choose one thing not said.
4. Write what its absence created.
5. Name the speech pattern from Day 1 that the next ten-day section should govern.
```

### 3. Seal the Mouth's Record

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: The Inventory
- Title: Seal the Mouth's Record
- Purpose: Choose one speech discipline and one important thing that needs to be said.
- Words: Repeat only what is seen, not what is heard. Do not repeat slander.
- Steps:
  1. Name the speech pattern that would most improve what your mouth creates if governed.
  2. Name the one thing not said that needs to be said before this flow closes.
  3. Write: In the next ten-day section, I will practice [discipline]. I will say [needed thing].

```text
3. Write: In the next ten-day section, I will practice [discipline]. I will say [needed thing].
```

### 4. The Governed Mouth Opens

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Governed Mouth
- Title: The Governed Mouth Opens
- Purpose: The tongue is the steering oar, not the pilot. The heart holds course; the tongue carries it out. This decan names the one discipline that keeps the oar from steering where the heart didn't direct it.
- Words: Be strong in your heart. Do not steer with your tongue.
- Steps:
  1. Review the five disciplines: pause, accuracy, relevance, timing, witness before speaking.
  2. For each, write whether it is currently operating in your speech.
  3. Choose one discipline for this ten-day section.
  4. Define the specific practice.
- Source note: Amenemope's image of the tongue as a steering oar places the problem clearly: an oar not controlled by the navigator creates the illusion of movement while taking the vessel in the wrong direction. Choose the discipline most absent.

```text
Words
"Be strong in your heart. Do not steer with your tongue."

Steps
1. Review the five disciplines: pause, accuracy, relevance, timing, witness before speaking.
2. For each, write whether it is currently operating in your speech.
3. Choose one discipline for this ten-day section.
4. Define the specific practice.
```

  2. Check whether the thing that needs to be said has been said.

### 7. The Mouth That Creates

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: The Mouth That Creates
- Title: The Mouth That Creates
- Purpose: The heart conceives before the tongue declares. Writing the true thing first is the heart's work — making it real through speech is the tongue's work. What is not fully conceived is not ready to be declared. This sitting completes the first half.
- Words: Your tongue is the plummet. Your heart is the weight. I open my mouth with care.
- Steps:
  1. Ask what your mouth has been creating across this flow.
  2. Name one specific, true, currently unspoken thing you will bring into the world by saying it.
  3. Write it first.
- Source note: The Memphite Theology describes creation as: heart conceives, tongue declares, world forms. The mouth that speaks before the heart has finished conceiving is interrupting the process. Writing the sentence first is the heart's work completed before the tongue takes over.

### 9. The Open Mouth

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Mouth That Creates
- Title: The Open Mouth
- Purpose: The flow closes with what was spoken that needed to be spoken, and with one minute of intentional silence. The silence is not the absence of speech — it is the space the governed mouth creates.
- Words: My mouth is open. My speech is governed. What I command, I create with care.
- Steps:
  1. Name the most significant speech pattern the inventory revealed.
  2. Name what the governance practice produced.
  3. Name what was spoken that needed to be spoken: I said [thing] on [day]. It is now in the world.
  4. Check whether the line is true before speaking it.
  5. Speak only the parts of the line that are true.
  6. Say, if true: My speech was not heated.
  7. Say, if true: My heart was not hasty.
  8. Say, if true: I said what needed to be said.
  9. Sit in intentional silence for one full minute.
- Source note: The Opening of the Mouth ceremony restored speech, eating, and breath after they had been closed. This close makes silence available — the governed mouth can be silent when silence is right. The one minute at the end is the practice of that.
- Completion labels:
  - Spoken
