## 24. The Living Record

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-living-record
- Tagline: What is recorded persists. Build the record of this decan.
- Overview: A 30-day record practice: turn the decan into a living account across day card, library, planner, journal, and physical line.
- Confidence: Draws on Kemetic logbooks, royal annals, autobiographies, and scribal record practice.
- Routing summary: Best for positive ḥꜣw practice, new-user feature integration, decan record keeping, journaling, guidance engagement, and users ready to use the full app suite intentionally.

### 1. Open the Day Card

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Enter the Record
- Title: Open the Day Card
- Purpose: The day card is the record's first entry — the dating before the work. Merer's logbook placed the date before naming the work because the work outside of time is not a record.
- Words: What occurred must be placed within its time. What is placed within its time becomes record. What becomes record persists.
- Steps:
  1. Open today's day card in ḥꜣw and read the Kemetic date, decan name, Ma'at principle, and cosmic context.
  2. Write the date down outside the app: Kemetic date, season, and decan name.
  3. Speak the line.
- Source note: Merer's logbook dated each entry before naming the work because work outside of time is not a record — it is an anecdote. The day card does the same: it places the decan's activity inside the Kemetic count before anything is added to it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The day card is the record's first entry — the dating before the work. Merer's logbook placed the date before naming the work because the work outside of time is not a record.

Words
"What occurred must be placed within its time. What is placed within its time becomes record. What becomes record persists."

Steps
1. Open today's day card in ḥꜣw and read the Kemetic date, decan name, Ma'at principle, and cosmic context.
2. Write the date down outside the app: Kemetic date, season, and decan name.
3. Speak the line.
```

### 2. Enter the Library

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Enter the Record
- Title: Enter the Library
- Purpose: One node grounds the decan record by explaining what this period is asking.
- Words: Better is the profession of the scribe than all professions. The one skilled in it is fit for office.
- Steps:
  1. Open the node library and choose the node that best matches the current decan quality or day-card principle.
  2. Read the node slowly, as a scribe reads to copy without distortion.
  3. Write one sentence in the physical record: The node that speaks to this decan is [name], and it means [one sentence].
- Source note: The House of Life was not a passive library. It preserved sacred texts so they could be read, copied, interpreted, and used.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

3. Write one sentence in the physical record: The node that speaks to this decan is [name], and it means [one sentence].
```

### 3. Set It in the Planner

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Enter the Record
- Title: Set It in the Planner
- Purpose: A scheduled act gives the decan quality a date and turns insight into work placed in time.
- Words: I have hoed emmer for you, I have plowed barley for you: the work of the season is placed in time.
- Steps:
  1. Open the planner and schedule one specific act this decan calls for.
  2. Review active flows and note one stalled or skipped place if it exists.
  3. Write in the physical record: Scheduled [what], on [date], because [decan quality].

### 4. Write in the Journal

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Build the Record
- Title: Write in the Journal
- Purpose: The journal is the papyrus — the private record from which the guidance system builds its account. What is written with honesty and specificity produces more accurate guidance than what is performed there with care for appearance. A specific record is more useful than a comprehensive one. Badges are signals that the entry has been recognized.
- Words: Their writing was their memory-priest. The pen was their child. Their names endured.
- Steps:
  1. Open the journal.
  2. Write at least three substantive sentences about what this decan has meant, what you actually did in Ma'at, what resisted the period, and one unanswered question.
  3. Notice any badges generated from the entry.
- Source note: Chester Beatty IV calls writing the memory-priest that keeps the name alive. The journal's entries are that priest — they build the account the guidance system works from. Honest and specific entries produce honest and specific guidance.

```text

Steps

2. Write at least three substantive sentences about what this decan has meant, what you actually did in Ma'at, what resisted the period, and one unanswered question.
3. Notice any badges generated from the entry.
```

### 5. Share in the Feed

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Build the Record
- Title: Share in the Feed
- Purpose: The record becomes communal when what others have placed on the wall is read and one small true thing is added.
- Words: O living ones who pass by: read what was done, speak the name, and let the record continue.
- Steps:
  1. Open the feed and read what others have shared for at least two minutes.
  2. Share one small record: a decan observation, flow completion, question, node insight, or day-card insight.
  3. Write: Shared [what]. Received from others: [one thing that stood out].
- Source note: Kemetic autobiographies were public inscriptions. The feed is not a tomb wall, but it does make a record visible to those passing through.

- Title: Complete an Alignment Check

- Steps:

  2. Review this decan so far: what has been consistent, and what has been disrupted?
  3. Write one specific change you will carry into Decan 3.

### 7. Enter Flow Studio

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Complete the Record
- Title: Enter Flow Studio
- Purpose: Flow Studio functions as the Per Ankh workshop: intention becomes a structured path on the calendar.
- Words: What the heart thinks, the tongue commands. The intention spoken becomes the flow that structures the work.
- Steps:
  1. Open Flow Studio.
  2. Generate a flow from an unresolved intention, review an active flow, or browse templates for the flow this decan points toward.
  3. Write: Flow Studio, Day 21: [what I generated, reviewed, or discovered]. The intention I carry forward is [one thing].
- Source note: The Per Ankh copied received texts and produced new ones. Flow Studio is the app's production room: received understanding becoming new structure.

### 8. Read the Ma'at Guidance

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: Complete the Record
- Title: Read the Ma'at Guidance
- Purpose: The guidance witnesses before it advises. It names what the record shows — not what you hope the record shows. Read it as a lector priest's consultation, not as a notification.
- Words: The guidance comes when the pattern has been seen. It witnesses before advising. It names without shame. It offers one act.
- Steps:
  1. Open the Ma'at guidance card, a recent guidance delivery, or the decan opening.
  2. Name the pattern it identified and the one act it recommends.
  3. If possible, complete the act today.
  4. If not, write what the right response is.
  5. Write: Ma'at guidance, Day 25: [what it said]. My response: [what I did or decided].
- Source note: The lector priest read the sacred texts aloud during ritual — the voice that activated what was written. The guidance reads the journal's record and names what is there. Reading it attentively, rather than skimming, completes the consultation.

### 9. Write the Decan's Closing Record

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Complete the Record
- Title: Write the Decan's Closing Record
- Purpose: The final journal entry and physical closing line bound the decan account so it can be returned to as a measured record.
- Words: I will put my annals among people and love of me among the gods. The record is real. The record persists.
- Steps:
  1. Open the journal and write the closing entry: date, decan, what was done, what was not done, what surprised you, and what continues.
  2. Read the physical record from Day 1 through Day 25.
  3. Write the closing line: In [Kemetic date], decan of [name], I [what I actually did in Ma'at]. The time was measured. The record is closed.
  4. Speak the final line.
- Source note: Merer's logbook ends with the final entry. The Palermo Stone closes each year with a marker. The Living Record closes when the decan is bounded and set down.
- Completion labels:
  - Record complete

## 25. Het-Heru

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: het-heru
- Tagline: The destroying eye and the goddess of joy are the same force.
- Overview: A 30-day cooling practice: name the hot force, meet its real need, and return it toward music, beauty, feast, and joy.
- Confidence: Draws on Sekhmet-Hathor transformation, the Feast of Drunkenness, and Hathor as Mistress of Joy.
- Routing summary: Best for joy, beauty, music, delight, creative heat, rage that has become self-perpetuating, and users who need transformation through abundance rather than suppression.

### 1. Find Your Sekhmet

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Eye Goes Out
- Title: Find Your Sekhmet
- Purpose: Sekhmet was sent by Ra for a legitimate reason, against a real threat. The force going further than intended is not a character flaw — it is what happens when the Eye has no beer. Name yours without assigning fault to its origin.
- Words: Sekhmet, the Powerful One, the Eye of Ra, went out and did not come back when called. She is the same force as Het-Heru before the beer.
- Steps:
  1. Read the core story: Ra sent the Eye as Sekhmet, she kept destroying after he called her back, and the gods flooded the field with red beer.
  2. Name your Sekhmet: resentment, ambition, grief, perfectionism, anger, or another force with its own momentum.
  3. Write: My Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It was sent out for [wound or purpose]. It is still going because [what sustains it].
- Optional steps:
  1. Name the beautiful thing this force used to make before it went too far.
- Source note: In the Book of the Heavenly Cow, Sekhmet's origin is Ra's wound and his legitimate anger. The force was right at the beginning. The problem arrived when it could not be recalled. This sitting names the force and its original purpose before deciding what the beer should be.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
Sekhmet was sent by Ra for a legitimate reason, against a real threat. The force going further than intended is not a character flaw — it is what happens when the Eye has no beer. Name yours without assigning fault to its origin.

Words
"Sekhmet, the Powerful One, the Eye of Ra, went out and did not come back when called. She is the same force as Het-Heru before the beer."

Steps
1. Read the core story: Ra sent the Eye as Sekhmet, she kept destroying after he called her back, and the gods flooded the field with red beer.
2. Name your Sekhmet: resentment, ambition, grief, perfectionism, anger, or another force with its own momentum.
3. Write: My Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It was sent out for [wound or purpose]. It is still going because [what sustains it].

Optional
- Name the beautiful thing this force used to make before it went too far.
```

### 2. The Scale of the Slaughter

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: The Eye Goes Out
- Title: The Scale of the Slaughter
- Purpose: Assess whether the force is still serving its original purpose or has developed destructive momentum.
- Words: She was wading through blood. She had gone beyond the point where a command could reach her.
- Steps:
  1. Ask whether your Sekhmet still responds to your intention, or whether it keeps moving after you try to stop.
  2. Name who or what has entered its path that you did not intend to harm.
  3. Write without shame: the force may have been appropriate at the beginning; the question is whether it is still under direction.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Name who or what has entered its path that you did not intend to harm.
3. Write without shame: the force may have been appropriate at the beginning; the question is whether it is still under direction.
```

### 3. Before the Beer

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: The Eye Goes Out
- Title: Before the Beer
- Purpose: Find the need underneath the destructive momentum. That need gives the beer its shape.
- Words: Ra looked at the field and did not meet Sekhmet with more force. He asked what would fill her.
- Steps:
  1. Ask what your Sekhmet is actually looking for beneath the destruction.
  2. Write: My Sekhmet is looking for [the underlying need].
  3. Carry that need into Decan 2. The beer must fill this need without continuing the destruction.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Write: My Sekhmet is looking for [the underlying need].
3. Carry that need into Decan 2. The beer must fill this need without continuing the destruction.
```

### 4. Find the Beer

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: The Red Beer
- Title: Find the Beer
- Purpose: The beer must be abundant. Ra did not pour one jar — he poured seven thousand. Whatever the transforming thing is, it must be given generously enough that the underlying need is actually met.
- Words: Seven thousand jars of beer were dyed red like blood. The force received what it could drink, and it was transformed.
- Steps:
  1. Name your beer: beauty, music, rest, connection, significance, touch, play, or another abundant good.
  2. Write: The beer for my Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It fills the actual need because [reason].
  3. Make it abundant. Ra did not pour one jar; he flooded the field.
- Source note: The myth names the scale: seven thousand jars, poured across the whole field of Lower Kemet. Transformation through a token gesture is not the Kemetic model. The beer that transforms must be given in the quantity that allows the need to be fully met.

```text
2. Write: The beer for my Sekhmet is [specific thing]. It fills the actual need because [reason].
3. Make it abundant. Ra did not pour one jar; he flooded the field.
```

### 5. Pour the Beer

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: The Red Beer
- Title: Pour the Beer
- Purpose: Do the abundant act that fills the underlying need without feeding the destruction.
- Words: The beer was poured before the face of the beautiful one. She drank and was content.
- Steps:
  1. Do the beer act today in its abundant version: real music, real beauty, real rest, real connection, or the specific thing you named.
  2. Do it as the main event, not as background or a smaller compromise.
  3. Afterward write one sentence: Did the field fill? Did Sekhmet drink?
- Completion labels:
  - Beer poured

### 6. Waking Up

- Event number: 6
- Flow day: 19
- Section: The Red Beer
- Title: Waking Up
- Purpose: Check whether the force has changed quality since the beer was poured.
- Words: When she awoke, she had forgotten the slaughter. She was the Golden One: the same Eye, transformed.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the Sekhmet from Day 1. Has it become less self-perpetuating?
  2. Write what actually happened, not what you hoped would happen.
  3. If it is still active, name what more beer would look like.
  4. If something shifted, name the first sign of Het-Heru.

### 7. The Sistrum Sounds

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Het-Heru Arrives
- Title: The Sistrum Sounds
- Purpose: The sistrum was not Het-Heru's symbol — it was her presence made audible. When it was shaken, the goddess arrived. This sitting makes music the main event, not the background.
- Words: Het-Heru, the Golden One, Mistress of Joy, Lady of the Dance. The sistrum sounds and she is present.
- Steps:

  2. Notice what the music does to the place where Sekhmet was.
  3. Afterward write one word about what you felt. That is the whole record.
- Source note: At Dendera, music was the primary act of worship, and the reception of that music was the offering returned. The sistrum sounds, and Het-Heru is present. The one word written after is the record of whether that presence arrived.

## 26. The Shore

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-shore
- Tagline: What is in your boat? Bring what you actually have.
- Overview: A 30-day exchange practice: inventory what you offer, make one honest exchange, and account for what returns.
- Confidence: The Shore draws on attested exchange practice, deben-weighted value, Amenemope balance language, and The Shipwrecked Sailor. This household flow reconstructs honest exchange at modern scale.
- Routing summary: Best for money, exchange, value, offers, sales, negotiation, resources, business, clients, and honest material return.

### 1. What Is in Your Boat?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: What Is in Your Boat?
- Title: What Is in Your Boat?
- Purpose: The sailor offered oil to the Prince of Punt — the main product of the island he was standing on. The Prince laughed. Honest inventory prevents that error.
- Words: Do well, and you will attain influence.
- Steps:
  1. Write an honest inventory of skills, labor, knowledge, and relationships you can offer now.
  2. Mark each item as active, cultivated but unused, or undeveloped.
  3. Finish: My boat is primarily full of ___.
- Source note: The Shipwrecked Sailor arrived with nothing and offered what the island already had. The exchange that worked was not the commercial offer — it was his honest presence and his true account of the situation. What is actually in your boat, not what you wish were there?

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

The sailor offered oil to the Prince of Punt — the main product of the island he was standing on. The Prince laughed. Honest inventory prevents that error.

Words
"Do well, and you will attain influence."

Steps
1. Write an honest inventory of skills, labor, knowledge, and relationships you can offer now.
2. Mark each item as active, cultivated but unused, or undeveloped.
3. Finish: My boat is primarily full of ___.
```

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: What Is in Your Boat?
- Title: What Is It Actually Worth?
- Purpose: Honest measure requires exchange value without inflation or deflation.
- Words: The measure is the eye of Re.
- Steps:
  1. Choose two or three items from your inventory.
  2. Write their honest exchange value, not the value you wish they had.
  3. Name where you are overvaluing or undervaluing them.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Words

2. Write their honest exchange value, not the value you wish they had.
3. Name where you are overvaluing or undervaluing them.
```

- Flow day: 9
- Section: What Is in Your Boat?
- Title: What Needs More Cultivation?
- Purpose: One offering can be cultivated so its real value increases.
- Words: Fortunate is a scribe skilled in his office.
- Steps:
  1. Name one offering that could become more valuable with focused development.
  2. Write the specific act that would increase its real value before the next ten-day section.
  3. Name how the offering would be measured more honestly after that cultivation.

- Steps:
  1. Name one specific exchange you intend to make: what you will offer, to whom, and what return you seek.
  2. Write what must be prepared before you make the offer.
  3. Confirm the offer is genuinely useful to that person.

### 5. Make the Exchange

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: The Shore
- Title: Make the Exchange
- Purpose: The shore is a physical place. The exchange must actually happen before this event is logged. A planned exchange is not the same as a completed one.
- Words: The Ape sits by the balance, while his heart is the plummet.
- Steps:
  1. Make one real exchange before logging this event.
  2. Record what was offered, what was received, and whether both sides received honest value.
  3. If it has not happened, name the blocker.
  4. Name the next concrete opening.
- Completion labels:
  - Exchanged

- Steps:
  1. Review the exchange.
  2. Ask whether you delivered what you promised.
  3. Ask whether the other side delivered what they promised.
  4. Name any correction, return, or completion still owed.

  3. Write what return still needs time before it can be measured.

### 8. Bread With an Easy Mind

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: The Return
- Title: Bread With an Easy Mind
- Purpose: Amenemope returns to this formulation three times — not as a preference for simplicity over wealth, but as a diagnostic. Anxious bread means something in the exchange was not at honest measure.
- Words: Better is bread when the mind is at ease than riches with anxiety.
- Steps:
  1. Eat something while doing this event.
  2. Ask whether you can eat without planning how to protect what you have or how to get more before it runs out.
  3. Ask whether this gain feels easy or anxious.
  4. Name what made it peaceful, or what made it uneasy.
- Source note: Amenemope's formulation is a diagnostic, not a moral preference. Bread eaten with an easy mind means the exchange was at honest measure. Bread eaten with anxiety means something went wrong in the gaining or the holding.

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Return
- Title: Make a Good Reputation
- Purpose: The account closes when what came to shore is weighed, told, and kept private where privacy is owed.
- Words: What came to the shore has been weighed and told.
- Steps:
  1. Write what you brought, what you exchanged, what returned, and what you will pass forward.
  2. Write the final line of the exchange story.
- Optional steps:
  1. Share the closing record only if it does not expose private details.

## 27. The Autobiography

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-autobiography

- Overview: A 30-day life-record practice: name capacities, works, gifts, and one honest claim supported by evidence.
- Confidence: Draws on Kemetic tomb and stela autobiography as a dated account of lived conduct.
- Routing summary: Best for legacy, life review, career arc, purpose, year review, accomplishments, unfinished work, and what comes next.

### 1. Survey the Capacities

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Survey the Years
- Title: Survey the Capacities
- Purpose: What you can actually do now is different from what you could do at twenty. This survey names the difference — the capacities that the years specifically built.
- Words: Say what is and do not say what is not.
- Steps:
  1. Look across your adult life.
  2. Name the capacities developed through years of work, failure, repetition, and attention.
  3. Write what you can actually do now, without inflation or deflation.
- Source note: The Kemetic autobiography opened with offices held and capacities demonstrated — not as a résumé but as a fact-check on what the years had actually produced. The capacities named here are the ones that required the full span to develop.

```text
3. Write what you can actually do now, without inflation or deflation.
```

- Steps:

  2. Keep it specific and chronological.
  3. Do not rank the works while listing them.

- Steps:
  1. Name what you gave beyond obligation: time, skill, provision, protection, knowledge, or care.
  2. Write specific acts, not general traits.
  3. Mark what was given quietly and would otherwise disappear from the record.

  4. Record what they confirmed or corrected, or record the privacy reason if the line stayed private.

## 28. The True Name

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-true-name
- Tagline: The account others gave you and the account the scale shows are not always the same.
- Overview: A 30-day private naming practice: measure a false account against the record, speak the accurate account, and act from it.
- Confidence: The True Name draws on Ren theology, Aset’s name-work, and declaration before the scale. This household flow frames identity as accurate account, not diagnosis.
- Routing summary: Best for identity, confidence, self doubt, false belief, shame, accurate account, and acting from truth through self-accounting.

### 1. What Account Are You Operating From?

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: False Account
- Title: What Account Are You Operating From?
- Purpose: The account that feels like "just the truth about me" is the one most worth examining. The account you chose to believe has friction; the one that feels like fact has been operating as premise long enough to lose its edges.
- Words: The first account is not your fault; it is the account to be measured.
- Steps:
  1. Complete: People like me generally ___. When it comes to ___, I am someone who ___. The thing I quietly believe about myself is ___.
  2. Do not judge it yet and do not share this material.
  3. If what surfaces feels larger than this flow can hold, pause the flow and seek support.
- Source note: The Ren — the name — was a constituent part of the person, as real as the body. A name given by someone else and accepted as your own still operates as your name. This sitting finds the name you have been living under and asks whether it belongs to you.

```text
1. Complete: People like me generally ___. When it comes to ___, I am someone who ___. The thing I quietly believe about myself is ___.
```

- Section: Declaration

- Purpose: The Memphite Theology places conception before declaration: the heart must know the form before the tongue can command it into existence. This sitting lets the heart conceive the accurate account in a specific current situation before the tongue is asked to declare it. The imagination must be specific to do its work.
- Words: The heart conceives; the tongue releases the word into form.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one specific current situation where the false account operates, not a general pattern.
  2. Imagine acting from the accurate account in specific detail.
  3. Write what that looks like before you attempt it.
- Source note: The heart conceives before the tongue commands — that is the Kemetic sequence. The imagination of acting from the accurate account in a specific situation is the heart's work before the declaration. What is vividly conceived with the heart's knowledge behind it is available to the tongue.

### 8. Speak the True Name

- Event number: 8
- Flow day: 25
- Section: Declaration
- Title: Speak the True Name
- Purpose: The Declaration of Innocence was spoken standing, before the divine court, aloud. Not written. Not thought. Spoken. This sitting follows the same form.
- Words: I am pure. I am pure. I am pure. I am pure.
- Steps:
  1. Stand before speaking. The declaration is not made while seated.
  2. Speak the accurate account aloud, then speak the evidence.
  3. Say the closing declaration.
  4. Record what it felt like.
- Source note: The Declaration was spoken in a hall, before 42 Assessors, standing. The posture was not ceremonial — it was the form in which the declaration carried weight. This sitting follows it.
- Completion labels:
  - Declared

## 29. The Living Text

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-living-text
- Tagline: The Library was written by those who came before. What do you add?
- Overview: A 30-day Library practice: read carefully, add reflection or connection, and close with a living mark.
- Confidence: The Living Text draws on scribal copying, colophon, variant, and commentary practices.
- Routing summary: Best for Library study, node entries, learning, questions, reflections, source connections, and adding insight to the living text.

### 1. Find the Entry That Calls You

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Read
- Title: Find the Entry That Calls You
- Purpose: The entry that arrests attention is already communicating something. Reading it fully — not the first paragraph, the whole entry — is the first act of the scribe who copies to transmit rather than to survey.
- Words: The Library lives when it is read.
- Steps:
  1. Open the Library and choose the entry that catches your attention.
  2. Read it fully.
  3. Write one new thought the entry opened in you.
- Source note: The Kemetic scribe copied what was assigned and what was sought out. The entry that arrests your attention is the one the Per Ankh is directing you to. Read it the way a scribe reads to copy: without assuming you know how it ends.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Words
"The Library lives when it is read."

Steps
1. Open the Library and choose the entry that catches your attention.
2. Read it fully.
3. Write one new thought the entry opened in you.
```

- Section: Read
- Title: Find the Entry You’ve Been Avoiding
- Purpose: Read the entry you keep circling or dismissing.
- Words: Avoidance is also a reading mark.
- Steps:
  1. Choose the Library entry you keep circling or dismissing.
  2. Read it fully.
  3. Write what the avoidance was about.
- Optional steps:
  1. Add one question the avoidance raises.

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Read
- Title: Find What You Don’t Understand
- Purpose: Confusion belongs in Your Insights. The unanswered question is as useful to the living record as what you can already answer.
- Words: No one is born wise.
- Steps:
  1. Choose an important entry you do not fully understand.
  2. Open the node and tap Your Insights.
  3. Write the exact unclear passage or concept as a question.
  4. Save it to the node so the gap remains visible in your own record.

### 4. Add Your First Reflection

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Contribute
- Title: Add Your First Reflection
- Purpose: A reflection is not a summary or a response. It is what the entry opened in you that the entry itself could not say — because the entry was written before you encountered it with your specific life.
- Words: Good advice may be found at the grindstones.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the node you chose on Day 1 and tap Your Insights.
  2. Add a reflection from your lived experience — what the entry opened in you that the entry itself could not say.
  3. Save it to the node.
- Optional steps:
  1. If it belongs to others, use Post to share it on your profile.
- Source note: Scribal annotations in Kemetic manuscripts were often incorporated into subsequent copies — the reader's gloss became part of the text. What your experience adds to the entry belongs in the record alongside the original.

### 9. Your Colophon

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Colophon
- Title: Your Colophon
- Purpose: The colophon closed with the scribe's mark: who was here, what was completed. Not a flourish — a record. This sitting produces the same mark on the living text.
- Words: Completed correctly; the living text includes this mark.
- Steps:
  1. Write your closing mark: what you read, what you added, and how the Library is richer because of it.
  2. Speak the closing line.
  3. Name reflections, questions, and connections without calling them comments.
- Optional steps:
  1. Share the final line if desired.
- Source note: The Kemetic colophon was a formal record, not a signature of pride. The scribe's name claimed engagement, not authorship. Your colophon does the same: not "I wrote this" but "I was here, I engaged with this, what I added belongs to it now."
- Completion labels:
  - Colophon written

## 30. The Clearing

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-clearing
- Tagline: Its fruit is something sweet, its shade is pleasant, and it reaches its end in a grove.
- Overview: A 30-day temperance practice: notice where heat drives action, create space before reply, and act from the cleared state.
- Confidence: The Clearing draws on Instruction of Amenemope’s teaching of the temperate person as a tree in a sunlit field.
- Routing summary: Best for reactivity, anger, conflict, heated speech, patience, temper, stillness, and creating space before response.

### 1. Find the Indoor Tree

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Indoor Tree
- Title: Find the Indoor Tree
- Purpose: The indoor tree is growing toward the available light, not toward the actual light. The direction it has been growing is visible in what it has been producing — and in what the heat has been costing.
- Words: The hot-headed man is like a tree grown in an enclosed space.
- Steps:
  1. Name one situation where heat or reaction drives your actions before the cleared state is available.
  2. Write the specific situation and what generates the heat.
  3. Record what the heat-driven pattern has cost.
- Source note: Amenemope's image is precise: the indoor tree loses its foliage in a moment because it grew toward whatever light was available, not toward the actual sun. The heat-driven response does the same — it goes toward the available outlet, not the right one.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose

Words
"The hot-headed man is like a tree grown in an enclosed space."

Steps
1. Name one situation where heat or reaction drives your actions before the cleared state is available.
2. Write the specific situation and what generates the heat.
3. Record what the heat-driven pattern has cost.
```

- Section: Indoor Tree
- Title: What Has the Heat Cost?
- Purpose: Name one concrete cost of the heat-driven pattern.
- Words: In a moment is its loss of foliage.
- Steps:
  1. Return to the situation from Event 1.
  2. Name one specific cost of the heat-driven pattern: relationship, work, energy, goodwill, or opportunity.
  3. Write what would change if the response came from steadiness instead.

```text
Words

2. Name one specific cost of the heat-driven pattern: relationship, work, energy, goodwill, or opportunity.
3. Write what would change if the response came from steadiness instead.
```

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Indoor Tree
- Title: Where Is the Clearing Already Present?
- Purpose: Find an existing domain of steadiness.
- Words: The truly temperate person sets himself apart.
- Steps:
  1. Name one domain where you already act from steadiness rather than heat.
  2. Write what this clearing produces.
  3. Name how it feels different from the heat-driven situation.

```text
Find an existing domain of steadiness.
```

### 4. Set Yourself Apart

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Sunlit Field
- Title: Set Yourself Apart
- Purpose: The act of setting yourself apart is physical and procedural, not just intentional. The clearing is found by getting out of the enclosure, not by deciding the enclosure is no longer one.
- Words: Set yourself apart before the response forms.
- Steps:
  1. Choose one concrete physical or procedural act that creates space before response: wait one hour, walk outside, write before sending, sleep on it, or consult the day card first.
  2. Do not use "try to be calmer" as the act.
  3. Write it as: Before responding to [situation], I will [specific act].
  4. Write when you will use it and what heat situation it interrupts.

## 31. The Wandering

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-wandering
- Tagline: I found, I found.
- Overview: A 30-day evening grief accompaniment: name the loss, search gently, and notice one capacity that remains or returns.
- Confidence: The Wandering draws on Aset and Nebet-Het’s search, lament, and finding in the Pyramid Texts. This household flow accompanies grief without treating it as something to win.
- Routing summary: Best for grief, loss, mourning, missing, memory, breakup, searching, finding, restoration, support, body, and evening practice. Never a substitute for crisis support.
- Safety note: If grief feels acute or unsafe, reach out to someone you trust, 988 in the US, or findahelpline.com internationally before returning here.

### 1. Name What Was Lost

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: The Search
- Title: Name What Was Lost
- Purpose: The loss named specifically is the loss that can be searched for. Aset did not search for "what was gone" — she searched for Ausar. The name begins the search. The water is provision for the body while it does this work.
- Words: My brother, for I have searched for you.
- Steps:
  1. Write the name of what was lost. If it is not a person, name it as specifically as one.
  2. Write one sentence about what it gave you that you cannot get elsewhere right now.
  3. Place water nearby.
  4. Drink it slowly after writing the name.
- Source note: Aset's search began with Ausar's specific identity — not with the category of loss but with the specific person. The specific name makes the search possible.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The loss named specifically is the loss that can be searched for. Aset did not search for "what was gone" — she searched for Ausar. The name begins the search. The water is provision for the body while it does this work.

Words
"My brother, for I have searched for you."

Steps
1. Write the name of what was lost. If it is not a person, name it as specifically as one.
2. Write one sentence about what it gave you that you cannot get elsewhere right now.
3. Place water nearby.
4. Drink it slowly after writing the name.
```

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: The Search
- Title: What Has the Loss Closed?
- Purpose: Name what grief has closed without trying to reopen it.
- Words: The ears are plugged; the mouth is shut.
- Steps:
  1. Name what grief has closed: mouth, ears, eyes, body.
  2. Write what no longer tastes, sounds, looks, or feels the same.
  3. Do not try to reopen it yet.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
Name what grief has closed without trying to reopen it.

Words
"The ears are plugged; the mouth is shut."

Steps
1. Name what grief has closed: mouth, ears, eyes, body.
2. Write what no longer tastes, sounds, looks, or feels the same.
3. Do not try to reopen it yet.
```

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: The Search
- Title: Begin the Search
- Purpose: Go toward what carries what was loved.
- Words: I have searched for you.
- Steps:
  1. Go to one place, object, memory, or person that carries what was loved.
  2. Look for what remains, not for what is gone to return.
  3. Write what you found.

```text
3. Write what you found.
```

- Section: The Finding
- Title: Where Do You Keep Looking?

- Words: The search has places it returns to.
- Steps:
  1. Notice where grief keeps returning: places, songs, objects, dreams, people, memories.
  2. Write where you keep looking.
  3. Name what draws you there.

### 5. The First Finding

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: The Finding
- Title: The First Finding
- Purpose: "I found, I found" — the double testimony. The finding is not the return of what was lost — it is what remains of the love in the places the search visited.
- Words: I found, I found.
- Steps:
  1. Name one thing the search has genuinely found.
  2. Write what remains of the love.
  3. Name where it has gone now.
- Source note: In the Pyramid Texts, the finding is announced twice: "I found, I found, said Isis; I have found, said Nephthys." The repetition is testimony. What is found by two witnesses has been genuinely found. This sitting names one genuine finding.

```text
2. Write what remains of the love.
3. Name where it has gone now.
```

- Words: I have found.

  2. Write what the calling-out showed.
  3. Name what searching alone did not show.

### 7. What the Loss Is Teaching

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: The Opening
- Title: What the Loss Is Teaching
- Purpose: Name one thing known from inside the loss.

- Steps:

  2. Do not frame the loss as for a reason.
  3. Write only what is true enough to hold tonight.

### 9. Stand Up

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: The Opening
- Title: Stand Up
- Purpose: The Pyramid Texts command to stand is not a demand to stop grieving — it is the voice of love that has searched, found, and now asks the found one to rise. The physical act is the event.
- Words: Stand up for me.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the line before standing.
  2. Stand physically before logging.
  3. Do one small act using a restored capacity: eat something wanted, listen to music, see something beautiful, or speak the name of what was lost to someone safe.
  4. Record the act.
- Source note: The command to stand in the Pyramid Texts is spoken by those who love the one who is down — Isis's command, Nephthys's command, Horus's command. The standing is invited by love that has found what it was looking for. This sitting receives that invitation and enacts it in one small act of the restored capacity.

## 32. The Khat

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-khat
- Tagline: Teti is sound because of his body.
- Overview: A 30-day body-care practice: listen to the body, answer with water, food, washing, rest, or movement, then record what changed.
- Confidence: The Khat draws on the physical restoration language of the Pyramid Texts: the body assembled, washed, fed, anointed, watered, and raised.
- Routing summary: Best for body care, tiredness, fatigue, sleep, water, food, movement, pain, tension, physical rest, exercise, and embodiment. Does not diagnose medical issues.
- Safety note: If your body is signaling something that may require medical attention, let this step support seeking care.

### 1. Listen to the Body

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Attend
- Title: Listen to the Body
- Purpose: The body is reporting something right now — not what you think it should be feeling, but what it is actually communicating in this moment. The inventory is not an assessment; it is the act of listening to a report the body has been trying to give.
- Words: Teti is sound because of his body.
- Steps:
  1. Sit or lie down.
  2. Move attention from feet to face.
  3. Write three things the body is communicating right now, without judging or correcting them.
- Source note: "Teti is sound because of his body." The soundness was located in the physical form — bones assembled, limbs collected, earth on the flesh cleared away. The khat is not the vessel for the other four parts; it is one of the five. This sitting attends to it as such.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
The body is reporting something right now — not what you think it should be feeling, but what it is actually communicating in this moment. The inventory is not an assessment; it is the act of listening to a report the body has been trying to give.

Words
"Teti is sound because of his body."

Steps
1. Sit or lie down.
2. Move attention from feet to face.
3. Write three things the body is communicating right now, without judging or correcting them.
```

- Purpose: Name what has accumulated on the body and clear one small thing.
- Words: The earth on your flesh has been cleared away.
- Steps:
  1. Name what has accumulated on the body: tension, fatigue, shallow breath, posture, clenching, or held stress.
  2. Do one immediate small clearing act.
  3. Record what changed, even if the change is small.

```text
3. Record what changed, even if the change is small.
```

- Purpose: Name one specific thing the body has been asking for.
- Words: Provision must be named before it can be received.
- Steps:
  1. Name one specific thing the body has been asking for and not receiving.
  2. Choose from sleep, water, food timing, movement, rest, touch, or less of something excessive.
  3. Write the smallest honest way to provide it in this ten-day section.

- Words: The body receives what is given with attention.
- Steps:
  1. At one meal today, eat with full attention and no screens.
  2. Record what the body received.
  3. Write what the body communicated during or after the meal.

### 5. The Anointing

- Event number: 5
- Flow day: 15
- Section: Provide
- Title: The Anointing
- Purpose: The anointing was not comfort — it was the mechanism of bodily agency: "you shall make him have control of his body." The Pyramid Texts anointed the forehead first because the forehead is the face the person presents to the world. The body that receives deliberate care is the body the person can actually inhabit.
- Words: Ointment returns attention to the body.
- Steps:
  1. After washing, apply oil, lotion, cream, or water to some part of the body with deliberate attention.
  2. Begin with the forehead if that is available to you.
  3. Record what the act returned to your relationship with the body.
  4. Keep body details private by default.
- Source note: The Pyramid Texts formula for anointing is specific: "you shall make it pleasant for him, wearing you; you shall make him have control of his body." The oil was the mechanism of control — the body that receives deliberate care is available to the person's intention.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Words
"Ointment returns attention to the body."

Steps
1. After washing, apply oil, lotion, cream, or water to some part of the body with deliberate attention.
2. Begin with the forehead if that is available to you.
3. Record what the act returned to your relationship with the body.
4. Keep body details private by default.
```

- Section: Provide

- Purpose: Set up rest the body can receive.
- Words: Rest is provision when the body can receive it.
- Steps:
  1. Before sleep, give the body a real rest setup: consistent hour, dark/cool/quiet space, five slow breaths, and no screens for 30 minutes if possible.
  2. Record the sleep quality in the morning.
  3. Name one setup element that helped or one that was missing.

### 7. Stand Up

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Move
- Title: Stand Up
- Purpose: The Pyramid Texts repeated this command because the rising is the primary physical act of restoration. Not the most dramatic, not the most strenuous — the most fundamental. The record follows the movement.
- Words: Stand up, repel your earth, clear away your dust, raise yourself.
- Steps:
  1. Speak the line before beginning to move.
  2. Begin moving deliberately for at least 20 minutes before logging.
  3. Walking, stretching, dancing, swimming, lifting, gardening, or physical cleaning all count if sustained and attended to.
  4. Record what the movement returned to the body.
- Source note: The command to stand appears throughout the Pyramid Texts corpus with the same urgency — it is the foundational physical act that all others depend on. The twenty-minute movement demonstrates that the body can still do what the rite commands.
- Completion labels:
  - Moved

## 33. The Oracle

- Source: mobile/lib/features/calendar/maat_decan_flow.dart
- Key: the-oracle
- Tagline: Thutmose placed himself in the shadow of the Great God and rested. The god came to him in the night. He awoke and acted.
- Overview: A 30-day dream-question practice: prepare one question, receive without forcing meaning, and test one sign through grounded action.
- Confidence: The Oracle draws on Kemetic dream incubation, dream books, and the Dream Stela of Thutmose IV.
- Routing summary: Best for dreams, sleep, oracle questions, guidance, signs, symbols, night records, waking impressions, uncertainty, and grounded action after interpretation.
- Safety note: If disturbing dream content appears, bring it to a qualified professional or trusted support before continuing.

### 1. Prepare the Reception Chamber

- Event number: 1
- Flow day: 1
- Section: Preparation
- Title: Prepare the Reception Chamber
- Purpose: Thutmose did not simply fall asleep near the Sphinx — he placed himself in the shadow deliberately. The preparation creates the conditions for reception.
- Words: I place myself in the shadow of the Great God.
- Steps:
  1. Before sleep, clear the space near your head.
  2. Place one small object there.
  3. Write your oracle question on paper.
  4. Set the paper under or beside the object.
- Source note: The Dream Stela records that Thutmose rested in the shadow of the Great God — a deliberate spatial act before the dream. The incubation at Kemetic temples followed the same logic: you position yourself within the field of divine presence and wait. The reception chamber is that positioning, made domestic.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text
Purpose
Thutmose did not simply fall asleep near the Sphinx — he placed himself in the shadow deliberately. The preparation creates the conditions for reception.

Words
"I place myself in the shadow of the Great God."

Steps
1. Before sleep, clear the space near your head.
2. Place one small object there.
3. Write your oracle question on paper.
4. Set the paper under or beside the object.
```

### 2. The Pre-Sleep Purification

- Event number: 2
- Flow day: 5
- Section: Preparation
- Title: The Pre-Sleep Purification
- Purpose: Purify before sleep and hold the question clearly.
- Words: The question is held clearly before sleep.
- Steps:
  1. Before sleep, wash your face and hands with cool water.
  2. Hold the oracle question clearly.
  3. Lie down with the question present.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Hold the oracle question clearly.
3. Lie down with the question present.
```

### 3. The Invocation

- Event number: 3
- Flow day: 9
- Section: Preparation
- Title: The Invocation
- Purpose: The oracle consultation was formal — a specific address to a specific presence, with a specific question, in a specific posture of receiving. This invocation follows that form.
- Words: The question is spoken once, then silence receives.
- Steps:
  1. Before lying down, address the deity, principle, Ba, Ka, Ma’at, or divine presence you are asking.
  2. Speak the question once, clearly and completely.
  3. Lie down.
  4. Do not speak again before sleep.
- Source note: Temple dream incubation was a formal consultation — not a vague hope for inspiration but a specific request made in a specific place with specific preparation. The invocation formalizes the request so that what the night sends can be understood as responsive to it.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

Words
"The question is spoken once, then silence receives."

Steps

2. Speak the question once, clearly and completely.
3. Lie down.
4. Do not speak again before sleep.
```

### 4. Record Upon Waking

- Event number: 4
- Flow day: 11
- Section: Reception
- Title: Record Upon Waking
- Purpose: The dream disperses immediately. Record before anything else — before the phone, before speaking, before the day's logic takes over.
- Words: The night record is written before interpretation.
- Steps:
  1. Immediately upon waking, reach for the notebook before the phone or speaking to anyone.
  2. Record images, words, feelings, colors, sequence, and atmosphere.
  3. Do not interpret yet.
- Source note: The temple dream-reader received the dreamer's account immediately upon waking — before the dream had been edited by the waking mind. The dream written in the first thirty seconds is more accurate than the dream reconstructed an hour later.

#### Canonical Detail Text

```text

2. Record images, words, feelings, colors, sequence, and atmosphere.
3. Do not interpret yet.
```

- Event number: 5

- Title: Distinguish Oracle from Ordinary
- Purpose: Mark possible oracle material without forcing meaning.
- Words: Not every dream is the oracle.
- Steps:
  1. Review the dream records.
  2. Mark possible oracle material: unusually vivid, direct address, clear relation to the question, or strong waking impression.
  3. Mark ordinary dreams honestly.

  2. Name what appeared more than once: figure, color, place, action, sound, feeling, or symbol.
  3. Keep the recurring element tied to the record.

### 7. The Kemetic Framework

- Event number: 7
- Flow day: 21
- Section: Interpretation
- Title: The Kemetic Framework
- Purpose: Interpret the recurring element through four grounded questions.
- Words: Interpretation serves action.
- Steps:
  1. Ask what the recurring element does.
  2. Ask what principle it carries.
  3. Ask how it relates to the oracle question.
  4. Ask what action it indicates without treating disturbing dream content as definitive truth.

- Words: The indication is named only as clearly as it was received.
- Steps:

  2. If unclear, say so honestly.
  3. Record the strongest impression received so far.

### 9. Act on What Was Received

- Event number: 9
- Flow day: 29
- Section: Interpretation
- Title: Act on What Was Received
- Purpose: The oracle completes when the action is taken. Thutmose cleared the sand from around the Sphinx. The dream that produces no action was not a consultation — it was a night's sleep.
- Words: He awoke and acted.
- Steps:
  1. If the oracle indicated a specific action, take that action before logging this event.
  2. If the oracle stayed unclear, do only the grounded action you can justify before logging this event.
  3. Write the complete oracle record: question, what the night sent, what it indicated or left unclear, and what action was taken.
- Source note: Thutmose's stela records the dream and then records that he commanded the sand to be cleared. The action is what closes the Stela's account — not the dream, not the interpretation, but the act taken in response.
- Completion labels:
  - Oracle complete
